On Lions’ density patch problem at a critical level of regularity
Stefan Škondrić, Alessandro Violini

Abstract
. In this article, we study Lions’ density patch problem in two space dimensions at critical regularity. We prove global existence, uniqueness, and stability for a fluid occupying a bounded Lipschitz region surrounded by vacuum and evolving according to the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, with initial velocity in B ˙ 2 , 1 0 ​ ( ℝ 2 ) \dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) . Moreover, we show that the Lipschitz regularity of the patch is preserved, and that its long-time dynamics is a rigid motion leading to the emergence of an asymptotic domain.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
76D03, 76D05, 76T10, 46E35, 35Q30

1.
Introduction

We study the evolution of a homogeneous incompressible fluid occupying a bounded region
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, surrounded by vacuum, and evolving according to the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. This configuration is commonly referred to as the
density patch problem
.

The region occupied by the fluid evolves in time into a family of domains
D_{t}
, describing the motion of the interface. This point of view naturally leads to a free-boundary formulation, where the evolution of the fluid is described through the motion of the domain
D_{t}
and its boundary.

An equivalent Eulerian formulation is obtained by introducing a density transported by the flow, which formally takes the form

(1.1)

\displaystyle\rho(t)=\mathbf{1}_{D_{t}}.

In this formulation, the problem can be described by the inhomogeneous incompressible Navier–Stokes system in

\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

(1.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\dive(\rho u\otimes u)-\nu\Delta u+\nabla P=0,\\
\partial_{t}\rho+\dive(\rho u)=0,\\
\dive u=0,\end{cases}

where
\rho\geq 0
denotes the density,
u
the velocity field,
P
the pressure, and
\nu
the viscosity. The system is supplemented with the initial data

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho(0,x)=\mathbf{1}_{D},\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x)\qquad\text{with }\dive u_{0}=0.\end{cases}

In this framework, the evolution of the interface
D_{t}
can be studied from a Lagrangian perspective. Indeed, the domains are given by

(1.4)

\displaystyle D_{t}=X(t,D),

where
X(t,x)
denotes the flow associated with the velocity field
u
. Since
u
is incompressible, i.e.
\dive u=0
, the flow
X(t,\cdot)
is measure-preserving, and therefore

(1.5)

\displaystyle|D_{t}|=|D|.

In particular, the evolution preserves the size of the patch, but this information alone does not provide any control on the geometry of the boundary. This naturally leads to the central question, raised by Lions in his monograph

Mathematical Topics in Fluid Mechanics

[
27
, p. 34]
, of whether the regularity of the domains
D_{t}
is preserved by the time evolution.

More precisely, starting from an initial domain
D
with a given geometric regularity, one may ask whether the transported domains
D_{t}
retain the same structure, or whether singularities may develop at the interface as time evolves. Since the domain
D
is transported by the flow
X(t,\cdot)
, its evolution ultimately depends on the regularity properties of the velocity field
u
and on the initial data
u_{0}
.

Our main result provides a positive answer to this question in a critical regularity framework. We establish existence, uniqueness, and stability of solutions, together with the propagation of Lipschitz regularity of the interface. In addition, we describe the long-time behavior of the flow, showing convergence towards a rigid motion and the emergence of an asymptotic domain.

Before stating a simplified version of our main result, we introduce the quantity

(1.6)

\displaystyle M:=\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)u_{0}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{D}u_{0}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

This quantity can be interpreted as the average momentum per unit mass inside the patch
D
. In particular, it has the same scaling as the velocity field
u
, and represents the average initial velocity of the fluid in
D
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D}
be the characteristic function of a domain

D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with Lipschitz boundary,
and let the initial velocity satisfy

(1.7)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\dive u_{0}=0.

Then there exists a unique global solution
(\rho,u)
to (
1.2
) satisfying the energy equality such that

(1.8)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u},\nabla^{2}u,\nabla P\in L^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),\qquad\nabla u\in L^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),\qquad\rho(t)=\mathbf{1}_{D_{t}},

where
D_{t}=X(t,D)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a Lipschitz domain for all
t\geq 0
, and
X(t,x)
is the flow associated with
u
.

Moreover, the quantity
M
is conserved in time, and there exists a bi-Lipschitz, measure-preserving map

X_{\infty}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

(1.9)

\displaystyle\|X(t,\cdot)-Mt-X_{\infty}(\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}\to 0\qquad\text{as }t\to\infty.

In particular, up to the rigid translation
Mt
, the domains
D_{t}
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the limiting domain
D_{\infty}\coloneqq X_{\infty}(D)
.

If, in addition,
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{\gamma}_{2,1}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then

\nabla u\in L^{1}((0,\infty);C^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. In this case, if the initial domain

D
is of class
C^{1,\gamma}
, then the evolved domains
D_{t}
remain of class

C^{1,\gamma}
for all
t\geq 0
.

The previous theorem shows that the evolution of the patch splits into a finite deformation and a rigid motion, namely a translation with constant velocity
M
, directed along the average of the initial velocity inside the patch. This decomposition highlights a striking rigidity in the long-time behavior of the flow. Indeed, the velocity converges exponentially fast to its spatial average inside the patch, namely

(1.10)

\displaystyle\|u(t)-M\|_{L^{2}(D_{t})}\lesssim\|u_{0}-M\|_{L^{2}(D)}\,e^{-\lambda t},

as proved in
Lemma

6.4
. The rate
\lambda
has the same scaling as the two-dimensional heat equation. This decay is a consequence of the dissipative structure of the system, which tends to damp out spatial variations of the velocity. At the same time, the conservation of the total momentum prevents the velocity from vanishing, and instead selects the constant vector
M
as the asymptotic state. As a consequence, the velocity becomes asymptotically constant and the motion is governed by the uniform drift
M
.

Moreover, the condition
\nabla u\in L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}
implies that the associated flow has finite total distortion. In particular, all the deformation of the domain is accumulated over time and remains finite. More precisely, after subtracting the translation
Mt
, the flow converges uniformly to a limiting map
X_{\infty}
, which admits the representation

(1.11)

\displaystyle X_{\infty}(x)=x+\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(u(t,X(t,x))-M\bigr)\,\,\mathrm{d}t,

see
Lemma

6.5
. In particular,
X_{\infty}
encodes the total deformation of the domain. As a consequence, the domains
D_{t}-Mt
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the limiting domain
D_{\infty}
, and no further deformation occurs as
t\to\infty
.

The key mechanism behind the previous result is the integrability condition

(1.12)

\displaystyle\nabla u\in L^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{\infty}\big),

which ensures that the associated flow has finite total distortion and allows one to control the evolution of the interface. This condition is critical with respect to the natural scaling of the system, and therefore represents the threshold for controlling the geometry of the flow.

We recall that the natural scaling of (
1.2
) is given by

(1.13)

(\rho_{\lambda},u_{\lambda},P_{\lambda})(t,x):=\big(\rho(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x),\;\lambda u(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x),\;\lambda^{2}P(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x)\big),\qquad\forall\lambda>0.

The space
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}
is invariant under the scaling (
1.13
). Moreover, one has the continuous embedding

(1.14)

\displaystyle\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}\hookrightarrow L^{2},

while the space
L^{2}
alone is not sufficient to ensure the propagation of the regularity of the patch.

This obstruction is already visible at the level of the linear heat equation: in two dimensions, the heat flow with initial data in
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}
satisfies (
1.12
)
whereas there exist explicit initial data
u_{0}\in L^{2}
for which this property fails. In particular, one cannot expect to obtain (
1.12
) for solutions to (
1.2
) starting from
L^{2}
initial data. As a consequence, the preservation of the Lipschitz regularity of the patch cannot hold in this setting.

For this reason,
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}
appears as the natural (critical) functional framework for the density patch problem with Lipschitz boundary.

Overview of the literature.

The mathematical analysis of (
1.2
) dates back to the works of Kazhikhov
[
23
]
and Simon
[
30
]
, who established global weak solutions under suitable assumptions on the density. A major breakthrough was achieved by Lions
[
27
]
, who constructed global weak solutions for initial velocity in
L^{2}
and bounded nonnegative initial density.

Well-posedness.

The well-posedness theory in the absence of vacuum has been extensively developed in a series of works, including Danchin
[
14
]
, Abidi
[
3
]
, Abidi–Gui–Zhang
[
1
]
, Abidi–Gui
[
2
]
, Danchin–Mucha
[
10
]
, Paicu–Zhang–Zhang
[
28
]
, Zhang
[
33
]
, and Danchin–Wang
[
12
]
. Recently, global well-posedness in two dimensions for critical data was established by Danchin
[
15
]
for
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}
, and shortly after by Hao, Shao, Wei and Zhang
[
21
]
for
u_{0}\in L^{2}
. The latter result was further extended to the full Leray–Hopf class in
[
31
]
. In this sense, the well-posedness theory in the absence of vacuum is by now well understood.

The analysis in the presence of vacuum for bounded densities is more delicate, since the momentum equation degenerates where the density vanishes. Danchin and Mucha
[
11
]
proved global well-posedness in two dimensions for
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and general bounded nonnegative densities. The extension to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
was obtained by Prange and Tan
[
29
]
and by Hao, Shao, Wei and Zhang
[
22
]
, under structural or geometric assumptions on the density, covering in particular density patch configurations. In both works, the initial velocity belongs in
H^{1}
.

Propagation of regularity.
The propagation of boundary regularity was first studied in a relaxed setting compared to that of Lions, where the initial density takes the form

(1.15)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(x)=\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{D}(x)+\rho^{\mathrm{out}}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{D^{c}}(x),\qquad\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{0},\rho^{\mathrm{out}}_{0}>c\geq 0.

In this framework, Liao and Zhang
[
24
,
25
]
proved the propagation of
W^{3,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
regularity of the interface for
p\in(2,4)
, first under a smallness assumption on the density jump and later without this restriction. See also Danchin and Zhang
[
13
]
for related results. Gancedo and García-Juárez
[
20
]
established the propagation of
C^{1+\gamma}
regularity of the interface for initial velocities with sharp Sobolev regularity
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with
s\in(\gamma,1)
, without any smallness assumptions.

In the presence of vacuum, that is when
\rho_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}=0
, only few results are available. Danchin and Mucha
[
11
]
proved the preservation of
C^{1,\gamma}
regularity of the boundary
D
for initial velocities
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see also
[
9
]
). More recently, Gancedo, García-Juárez, and Luna-Velasco
[
19
]
extended this result to initial data
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
s\in(\gamma,1)
.

Theorem

1.1
advances the theory initiated in
[
11
]
by establishing global well-posedness together with the propagation of boundary regularity in the critical scaling-invariant framework, in the whole space and in the presence of vacuum. In addition, the Lipschitz control (
1.12
) provides a quantitative description of the flow and allows us to characterize its long-time asymptotic behavior. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result in the literature establishing such asymptotic properties for density patches.

Conventions on the Notation.

We write
A\lesssim B
to denote an inequality up to a multiplicative constant whose dependence is not relevant. When we need to emphasize the dependence on a parameter
p
, we write
A\lesssim_{p}B
. The measure of integration is omitted whenever it is clear from the context. Similarly, we omit the domain in norms when no ambiguity arises.

For simplicity, except when studying the asymptotics, we set
\nu=1
.

1.1.
Notion of solutions and Main results

Consider the following initial data:

(1.16)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad 0\leq\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

where
L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denotes the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
consisting of weakly divergence-free vector fields. Under these assumptions, one can introduce the classical notion of weak solution satisfying the energy inequality, namely a Leray–Hopf-type weak solution.

Definition 1.2

(Weak solution)
.

A pair
(\rho,u)
is called a weak solution of (
1.2
)
with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying (
1.16
) if the following properties hold:

(i)

The solution satisfies the integrability conditions

(1.17)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}u\in L^{\infty}\big((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),\qquad\rho\in L^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big),\qquad\nabla u\in L^{2}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big).

(ii)

The pair
(\rho,u)
is a distributional solution of (
1.2
), in the following sense:

–

For every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}\big)
,

(1.18)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dx\,dt=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}\,\varphi(0,x)\,dx.

–

For every
\varphi\in C_{c,\sigma}^{\infty}\big([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
,

(1.19)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,\partial_{t}\varphi\cdot u+\rho\,u\otimes u:\nabla\varphi\,dx\,dt-\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla u:\nabla\varphi\,dx\,dt=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}u_{0}\cdot\varphi(0,x)\,dx.

–

For every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}\big)
and for almost every
t\in(0,\infty)
,

(1.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(t)\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dx=0.

(iii)

The energy inequality holds for every
t\geq 0
:

(1.21)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t,x)\,|u(t,x)|^{2}\,dx+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u(s,x)|^{2}\,dx\,ds\;\leq\;\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,|u_{0}(x)|^{2}\,dx.

Solutions of (
1.2
) with initial data in
L^{2}
typically satisfy higher-order energy estimates, at the expense of a singular behavior near
t=0
, reflecting the parabolic regularization of the system. In particular, weak solutions constructed via approximation schemes (and, in a suitable sense, mild solutions associated with the heat semigroup) satisfy additional
a priori
bounds. It is natural to expect that such estimates can be propagated to the limit.

Let
u
be a weak solution in the sense of
Definition

1.2
. For a (sufficiently regular) function
v
, we define the
material derivative
along
u
by

(1.22)

\displaystyle\dot{v}:=D_{u}v:=\partial_{t}v+u\cdot\nabla v,

which represents the rate of change of
v
along the flow generated by
u
.

For
\eta\in[0,1]
and
t\geq 0
, we introduce the time-weighted energy functionals

(1.23)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A^{\eta}_{0}(t,v)&\coloneqq\|\sqrt{\rho}\,v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,\\
A^{\eta}_{1}(t,v,u)&\coloneqq t^{1-\eta}\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{1-\eta}\|\nabla^{2}v,\,\nabla P,\,\sqrt{\rho}\,\dot{v}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,\\
A^{\eta}_{2}(t,v,u)&\coloneqq t^{2-\eta}\bigl(\|\sqrt{\rho}\,\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\bigr)+\int_{0}^{t}s^{2-\eta}\|\nabla\dot{v}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,\\
A^{\eta}_{3}(t,v,u)&\coloneqq t^{3-\eta}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{3-\eta}\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

If
u=v
, we simply write
A_{i}^{\eta}(t,u)
for
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

Definition 1.3

(Immediately strong solution)
.

A pair
(\rho,u)
is called an
immediately strong weak solution
of (
1.2
)
with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying (
1.16
) if
(\rho,u)
is a weak
solution and there exists a constant
C_{(\rho,u)}(u)
such that

(1.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u)\leq C_{(\rho,u)}(u).

For a detailed discussion of this class of solutions and related equivalent notions, we refer to
[
6
]
.

We now focus on the case of density patches, namely

(
L^{2}
-patch)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D},

where
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain. In this setting, we establish the following existence result. To the best of our knowledge, all the properties listed below are new in this level of generality.

Theorem 1.4

(Existence for
L^{2}
data)
.

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
be as in (

L
2

-patch
). Then there exists an immediately strong weak solution
(\rho,u)
to (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
.

Moreover, the following properties hold:

(i)

Continuity in time:

(1.25)

\displaystyle\rho u\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\qquad\rho\in C([0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

(ii)

Energy equality: the identity (
1.21
) holds with equality.

(iii)

Conservation of momentum: for almost every
t\geq 0
,

(1.26)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t,x)\,u(t,x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,u_{0}(x)\,dx.

(iv)

Propagation of the support: there exists a constant
C>0
such that for almost every
t\geq 0
,

(1.27)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\rho(t)\subseteq D+B_{C\sqrt{t}}(0).

We refer to the next subsection for a discussion of the result and an outline of the proof. For the moment, we only point out that properties (iii) and (iv) hold for general immediately strong weak solutions (see Section 3), while (i) and (ii) require an explicit construction of the solution (see Section 4).

Combining the estimates
A_{i}^{0}(t,u)
is not sufficient to show that
\nabla u\in L^{1}(0,\infty;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. As discussed above, the obstruction already appears at the level of the heat equation, which does not allow one to control critical quantities in
L^{1}
in time starting from
L^{2}
initial data.

For this reason, we restrict to the case of Besov initial data:

(1.28)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D}.

In this setting, we establish global existence and uniqueness of Lipschitz solutions.

Theorem 1.5

(Well-posedness for
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}
data)
.

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
be as in (
1.28
). Then there exists an immediately strong weak solution
(\rho,u)
to (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying all the properties of
Theorem

1.4
. In addition, the following quantity is bounded:

(1.29)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+t^{3/4}\|\nabla\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\sqrt{t}\,\|\nabla\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right)\,\,\mathrm{d}t,

and
\sqrt{t}\,\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\to 0
as
t\to 0
.

Let
\bar{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and let
(\bar{\rho},\bar{u})
be an immediately strong weak solution arising from
(\rho_{0},\bar{u}_{0})
. Fix
t>0
. Then there exist a function
\gamma\in L^{1}(0,\infty)
and a constant
C>0
such that

(1.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in(0,t)}\|\bar{\rho}(s)\,\left(\bar{u}(s)-u(s)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\|\rho_{0}\,\left(\bar{u}_{0}-u_{0}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\exp\!\Bigl(C\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\,\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigr).

In particular, if
\bar{u}_{0}=u_{0}
, then
(\bar{\rho},\bar{u})=(\rho,u)
almost everywhere in
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We emphasize that the estimate on each of the critical quantities in (
1.29
) relies crucially on the assumption
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, as discussed in Section 5. The stability estimate (
1.30
) requires one of the solutions to be the Besov solution constructed above, while the other is only assumed to be an arbitrary immediately strong weak solution. For this reason, the result can be interpreted as a weak–strong uniqueness statement.

In the case of more regular initial data or a more regular initial patch, a slight adaptation of the previous theorem naturally yields the following proposition, which, for the first time, covers the critical level of regularity for
C^{1,\gamma}
patches with
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be the Besov solution constructed in
Theorem

1.5
. Then
1
1

1

Here,
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denotes the space of bounded, continuous functions vanishing at infinity.

(1.31)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

In particular, for every
t\geq 0
, the associated flow satisfies
X(t)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and if
D
is a
C^{1}
domain, then
D_{t}
is also of class
C^{1}
.

If in addition
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{\gamma}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then

(1.32)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{C^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Consequently, for every
t\geq 0
one has
X(t)\in C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and if
D
is a
C^{1,\gamma}
domain, then
D_{t}
remains a
C^{1,\gamma}
domain for all
t\geq 0
.

Compared to
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
, where the patch regularity is preserved provided
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
s>\eta
,
Theorem

1.6
establishes the end point case
s=\eta
in the Besov setting.

Once the global Lipschitz estimate is established, the total distortion of the flow map is finite:

(1.33)

L_{\infty}:=\sup_{t>0}\|\nabla X(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\exp\!\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\right)<\infty.

As a consequence, the asymptotic behavior can be studied.

Theorem 1.7

(Asymptotics)
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be the Besov solution constructed in
Theorem

1.5
. Then

(1.34)

\displaystyle\|u(t)-M\|_{L^{2}(D_{t})}\leq\|u_{0}-M\|_{L^{2}(D)}\,e^{-\lambda t},\qquad\lambda\coloneqq\frac{\nu}{C_{D}^{2}L_{\infty}^{2}},

where
C_{D}
is the Poincaré constant of
D
,
M
is the average momentum defined in (
1.6
), and
L_{\infty}
is the upper bound on the distortion defined in (
1.33
).

Moreover, there exists a measure-preserving map
X_{\infty}
such that

(1.35)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl\|(X(t,\cdot)-Mt)-X_{\infty}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}=0.

Furthermore, for every
x\in D
, we have

(1.36)

\displaystyle X_{\infty}(x)=x+\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(u(t,X(t,x))-M\bigr)\,\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
u(t,X(t,x))-M\in L^{1}(0,\infty)
for every
x\in D
.

In the next subsection, we outline the main difficulties and the strategy of the proofs of
Theorems

1.4
,
1.5
and
1.7
.

1.2.
Main difficulties and ideas

In this section, we discuss the main difficulties of the problem and outline some of the key ideas of the analysis.

The analysis relies on three key mechanisms: a localized, scaling-critical substitute for Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities; a gluing procedure based on atomic decompositions, which allows one to recover strong structural properties of weak solutions; and a refined analysis of Lipschitz patches, which yields local-in-time Lipschitz control of the velocity and, when combined with a Galilean transformation removing the rigid motion component, leads to a global-in-time control.

Lack of integrability outside of the patch and a dynamic Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality.

The first issue we address is the lack of integrability of the velocity field outside the patch.
Indeed, the energy estimates only control

(1.37)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(X(t,D))},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))},

but they do not provide any bound on
u(t)
in
L^{p}
outside the patch, for any
p\in[1,\infty]
.
In dimension three, this difficulty can be bypassed thanks to the Sobolev embedding

(1.38)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\;\lesssim\;\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

which yields integrability of
u(t)
directly from the dissipation.
In dimension two, however, the critical embedding only gives

(1.39)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{BMO(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\;\lesssim\;\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which does not imply any
L^{p}
-integrability at the critical level.

To overcome this obstacle, Prange and Tan
[
29
]
, and later Hao, Shao, Wei and Zhang
[
22
]

(see in particular
[
22
, Lemma A.2]
), proved that

(1.40)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\;\lesssim\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

under suitable geometric assumptions on the density.

However, this estimate is not compatible with the critical scaling, since the two terms on the right-hand side scale differently. As a consequence, it cannot be used in a critical framework.

Our goal is therefore to obtain a critical substitute for (
1.40
).
The price we pay is that the estimate holds only locally in space, namely in a
\sqrt{t}
-neighborhood of the initial patch:

(1.41)

\displaystyle D_{R,t}:=D+B_{R\sqrt{t}}(0).

The mechanism relies on two ingredients.

First,
Proposition

3.9
shows that (
1.41
) is the natural region where the dynamics takes place. Indeed, there exists
C>0
such that

(1.42)

\displaystyle X(t,D)=\operatorname{supp}\rho(t)\subseteq D_{C,t}.

Second,
Corollary

3.6
yields that for any measurable
v
, any
t>0
, any
p\in[2,\infty)
, and any
R>0
,

(1.43)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\,\|v\|_{L^{p}(D_{R,t})}\;\lesssim_{D,R,p}\;t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

The implicit constant deteriorates as
p\to\infty
and
R\to\infty
.
This inequality is obtained by exploiting the transport structure of (
1.2
), together with the Lipschitz regularity of the domain
D
and a
\rho(t)
-weighted version of the Poincaré inequality.

Together, (
1.42
) and (
1.43
) show that the relevant dynamics is effectively confined to regions of the form (
1.41
), where one recovers critical integrability. This provides a localized, time-weighted substitute for the classical Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality adapted to the moving domain. Indeed, combining (
1.43
) and (
1.42
), we obtain that for any measurable function
v
and any
p\in[2,\infty)
,

(1.44)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{p}(X(t,D))}\leq\|v\|_{L^{p}(D_{R,t})}\;\lesssim\;t^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}\,\|v\|_{L^{2}(X(t,D))}.

This estimate should be compared with the classical Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality on Lipschitz domains, which states that for any Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and any
p\in[2,\infty)
,

(1.45)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\;\lesssim\;\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{p}}.

If the flow
X
were Lipschitz, then
X(t,D)
would remain a Lipschitz domain uniformly in time, and (
1.45
) applied with
\Omega=X(t,D)
would yield (
1.44
) via a suitable Young inequality with time weights. However, in our setting the flow is not known to be Lipschitz (we work with immediately strong solutions), so this argument is not available. Moreover, (
1.44
) provides control of
v
on the larger set
D_{R,t}\supset X(t,D)
, a feature which is crucial in the analysis. Thus, (
1.44
) can be viewed as a substitute for (
1.45
) compatible with the available regularity: although weaker in structure, it preserves the correct scaling and encodes the same interpolation mechanism through time weights.

Having established this substitute, we now present some applications. First, it yields new
L^{p}
estimates for quantities such as
\rho u
and
\rho\dot{u}
.
Since
\rho(t)=\mathbf{1}_{X(t,D)}
, choosing
p=4
and
v=\dot{u}(t)
in (
1.44
), we obtain

(1.46)

\displaystyle\|\rho\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\;\lesssim\;t^{\frac{1}{4}}\,\|\nabla\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+t^{-\frac{1}{4}}\,\|\rho\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Multiplying by
t^{3/4}
, integrating in time, and using the bounds encoded in
A_{1}^{0}(t,u)
and
A_{2}^{0}(t,u)
, we obtain

(1.47)

\displaystyle t^{3/4}\,\|\rho\dot{u}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}[0,\infty),

which is a scale-invariant estimate.

Similarly, choosing
v=u(t)
and
p=2
, we obtain for every
R>0

(1.48)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(D_{R,t})}\;\lesssim\;t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Compared to (
1.40
), both terms on the right-hand side now scale in the same way.

Another crucial application of (
1.44
) is uniqueness.
Consider two sufficiently regular solutions
(\rho_{1},u_{1})
and
(\rho_{2},u_{2})
with the same initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
, and set
(\delta\rho,\delta u)=(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2},u_{1}-u_{2})
. Then, for almost every
t>0
(see
Theorem

A.5
),

(1.49)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{1}}\,\delta u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\delta u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,ds\leq-\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,\delta u\!\otimes\!\delta u:\nabla u_{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,\delta u\cdot\dot{u}_{2}.

The first term on the right-hand side can be handled by Gronwall, since

(1.50)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,\delta u\!\otimes\!\delta u:\nabla u_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho_{1}}\,\delta u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\|\nabla u_{2}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,ds,

and
\nabla u_{2}\in L^{1}((0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

The second term is more delicate, due to the lack of integrability of
\delta u
and
\dot{u}_{2}
. However, thanks to (
1.42
), there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for almost every
s>0
,

(1.51)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\rho_{1}(s)\subseteq D_{C_{1},s},\qquad\operatorname{supp}\rho_{2}(s)\subseteq D_{C_{2},s}.

Setting
C=\max\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
, we obtain

(1.52)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,\delta u\cdot\dot{u}_{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{D_{C,s}}\delta\rho\,\delta u\cdot\dot{u}_{2}.

In this localized region we can exploit (
1.43
). For instance, applying it with
v=\delta u(s)
yields

(1.53)

\displaystyle\|\delta u(s)\|_{L^{2}(D_{C,s})}\;\lesssim\;s^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\nabla\delta u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\sqrt{\rho_{1}(s)}\,\delta u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

This provides the correct structure to close the Gronwall argument.
The key point is that the dynamics can be localized in
\sqrt{t}
-neighborhoods of the initial patch, where critical integrability is recovered; combined with
\dot{H}^{-1}
-type stability for the density, this yields uniqueness.

Existence by gluing

The second difficulty is the construction of sufficiently regular weak solutions.
To the best of our knowledge, the only existence result for initial data
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in the presence of vacuum can be found in
[
21
, Section 4]
, where the existence of a distributional solution to (
1.2
) is established.

Their approach (see also
[
11
]
) relies on a lifting procedure for the initial density. More precisely, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, one considers the regularized data

(1.54)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{n}=\rho_{0}+\frac{1}{n},

which is bounded away from vacuum, and constructs a unique solution
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
associated with the initial data
(\rho_{0}^{n},u_{0})
. One then shows that the sequence
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
is sufficiently compact to pass to the limit in the distributional formulation, yielding a solution
(\rho,u)
of (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
.

In
Proposition

4.1
, we present a proof based on a similar lifting scheme which, thanks to the critical control provided by (
1.43
) and the a priori bounds encoded in
A_{3}^{0}(t,u_{n})
, yields a distributional solution that additionally satisfies the energy inequality and enjoys the following convergences in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr)
:

(1.55)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u,\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}u,\qquad\dot{u}_{n}\to\dot{u},\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}.

By identifying the limits in all nonlinear terms and using the lower semicontinuity of weak convergence, we show that
(\rho,u)
is not only a Leray–Hopf solution, but in fact an immediately strong solution.

However, the immediately strong solution obtained via lifting does not possess sufficient structure to establish uniqueness and long-time asymptotics. In particular, one needs the strong continuity

(1.56)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}u\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

which is closely related to the energy equality. Both (
1.56
) and the energy equality hold at the level of the approximating sequence
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
, but cannot be directly passed to the limit, since one cannot improve the convergence of
\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n}
to
\sqrt{\rho}u
in a strong enough sense (see
Remark

4.2
). A similar obstruction appears when considering mollification of the initial data.

To overcome this difficulty, we exploit a decomposition of the initial data. More precisely, we write

(1.57)

\displaystyle u_{0}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{0,j},

where the partial sums converge strongly to
u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and each atom
u_{0,j}
is divergence free and belongs to the subcritical space
\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
\eta\in(0,1)
. Such a decomposition can be obtained, for instance, via a Littlewood–Paley decomposition or through an atomic decomposition in the spirit of
[
26
]
via real interpolation. In this work, we adopt the latter approach.

For each atom
u_{0,j}
, we then consider the linearized system transported by
(\rho,u)
, where
(\rho,u)
is the solution constructed via lifting:

(1.58)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\rho u_{j})+\dive(\rho u\otimes u_{j})+\nabla P_{j}=\Delta u_{j},\\
\dive u_{j}=0,\\
u_{j}(0)=u_{0,j}.\end{cases}

In
Lemma

4.5
, we prove that (
1.58
) admits a solution such that
\rho u_{j}\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and satisfies the corresponding energy equality. This relies on the additional regularity of the initial data, namely
u_{0,j}\in\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

We then define the partial sums

(1.59)

\displaystyle u_{J}:=\sum_{|j|\leq J}u_{j},

which, being finite sums of solutions to (
1.58
), inherit the same properties. We next study the convergence
u_{J}\to u
.

Since
u_{J}-u
still solves (
1.58
), we have

(1.60)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(u_{J}-u))+\dive(\rho u\otimes(u_{J}-u))+\nabla(P_{J}-P)=\Delta(u_{J}-u),

and testing (
1.60
) with
(u_{J}-u)
yields, for almost every
t>0
,

(1.61)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho(t)}(u_{J}(t)-u(t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla(u_{J}(s)-u(s))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}\|u_{0,J}-u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Since the right-hand side converges to zero as
J\to\infty
(by (
1.64
)), we deduce the strong convergences

(1.62)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}u_{J}\to\sqrt{\rho}u\quad\text{in }C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\qquad\nabla u_{J}\to\nabla u\quad\text{in }L^{2}((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

These convergences are sufficient to pass to the limit in the energy equality for
u_{J}
, thereby obtaining the energy equality for
u
.

We emphasize that testing (
1.60
) with
u_{J}-u
is not immediate, due to the lack of decay of
u_{J}-u
as
|x|\to\infty
. The corresponding justification, including admissibility of test functions, is addressed at the end of Section 3 and in Section 7.

Finally, we believe that this construction is robust and may be adapted to other contexts.

Galilean Transform and faster decays for negative Sobolev data

The impossibility of having
\nabla u\in L^{1}((0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
for initial data
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
can already be observed at the level of the heat equation. Indeed, one has

(1.63)

\displaystyle\|\nabla(e^{t\Delta}u_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\frac{1}{t}\,\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where
e^{t\Delta}
denotes the heat semigroup. Clearly, the function
t\mapsto\frac{1}{t}
is not integrable near
t=0
, nor at infinity. To obtain time-integrability of
\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, the optimal space for the heat equation is
\dot{B}^{-1}_{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(
[
4
, Theorem 2.34]
). However,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\not\subset\dot{B}^{-1}_{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and if
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{-1}_{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then the corresponding solution
e^{t\Delta}u_{0}
does not have finite energy.

For this reason, the natural choice is the space
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which captures both finite energy and the critical integrability properties. To show that initial data in
\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
generate Lipschitz solutions, several techniques can be used. Among them is the method of
dynamic interpolation
.

We briefly sketch this idea. Let
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, for every
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists a sequence
(u_{0,j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that

(1.64)

\displaystyle u_{0}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{0,j},\qquad c_{j}:=2^{-j/2}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+2^{j/2}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\qquad\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{j}\leq 2\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

and each
u_{0,j}\in\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

For each
u_{0,j}
, we denote by
u_{j}
the solution to the heat equation with initial data
u_{0,j}
. By the regularization properties of the heat semigroup, one has

(1.65)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\min\Bigl\{t^{\frac{\eta}{2}-1}\,\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\;t^{-\frac{\eta}{2}-1}\,\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\Bigr\}.

The idea is now to exploit both decay regimes in (
1.65
): for small times we use the estimate involving the positive Sobolev norm
\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, while for large times we use the one involving the negative Sobolev norm
\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. This is implemented by splitting the time integral:

(1.66)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2^{-2j/\eta}}\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt+\int_{2^{-2j/\eta}}^{\infty}\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt

(1.67)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{0}^{2^{-2j/\eta}}t^{\frac{\eta}{2}-1}dt+\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{2^{-2j/\eta}}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{\eta}{2}-1}dt

(1.68)

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-j/2}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+2^{j/2}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=c_{j}.

Since
(c_{j})\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
, summing over
j
and using the linearity of the heat equation yields

(1.69)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{j}\leq 2\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Compared to other techniques for obtaining Lipschitz regularity,
dynamic interpolation
can also be adapted to PDEs with nonlinearities, as in the case of the Navier–Stokes system. This was observed by Danchin in
[
15
]
. We take inspiration from his work and apply a similar idea in the vacuum case. Since
u_{0}\in\dot{B}^{0}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can use
Theorem

1.4
to construct a solution
(\rho,u)
from patch data
(\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D},u_{0})
. We then apply the atomic decomposition (
1.64
) and, for each atom, consider
u_{j}
as the solution to the linear system (
1.58
) with initial data
u_{0,j}
.

We then prove the estimates in (
1.65
) with a constant
C
independent of
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, and by repeating the time-splitting argument described above, we obtain
\nabla u\in L^{1}((0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, using that the series
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{j}
converges to
u
.

Since the existence of
(\rho,u)
is already established in
Theorem

1.4
, the main difficulty is to prove (
1.65
). In our case, the estimates corresponding to positive Sobolev regularity in
\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
follow from the bounds on the functionals
A_{i}^{\eta}(t,u_{j},u)
established in Section 2. These estimates hold for any nonnegative initial density and do not rely on the patch structure.

It remains to study the decay of
\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
in terms of negative Sobolev initial data, and here the patch structure plays a crucial role. To better explain the difficulty, let us first consider the homogeneous case
\rho_{0}(x)=1
. In that setting, negative Sobolev regularity is propagated, and by interpolating

(1.70)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(2t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\,t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{j}(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\qquad\|\nabla u_{j}(2t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\,t^{-1}\|u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

one obtains

(1.71)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(2t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\,t^{-1+\frac{\eta}{2}}\|u_{j}(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Moreover, by the smoothing effect of the heat semigroup, one has

(1.72)

\displaystyle\|u_{j}(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim t^{-\eta}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Inserting (
1.72
) into (
1.71
), one recovers the desired decay estimate. The previous interpolation argument can also be suitably adapted to the case of initial densities bounded away from zero, namely when
\rho_{0}(x)\geq c>0
. Unfortunately, this is no longer possible in the patch case
\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D}
. Indeed, for
t>0
one generally has
u_{j}(t)\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and therefore one cannot rely on
\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
estimates as in the homogeneous case. For this reason, we cannot proceed as above and must develop a different approach.

As a first step, we perform a careful time shift and prove that

(1.73)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(2t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\,t^{-\frac{3}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

where the presence of
\sqrt{\rho}
is crucial. We now study the decay of the
L^{2}
-norm of
\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}
in terms of negative Sobolev initial data. We proceed by duality, introducing the backward system on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(1.74)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{s}(\rho w)+\dive(\rho u\otimes w)+\nabla Q=-\Delta w+\rho u_{j},\\
\dive w=0,\\
w(t)=0.\end{cases}

Then we have

(1.75)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,w(0,x)\cdot u_{0,j}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Since
D
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, one has

(1.76)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{D}\in\mathcal{M}\bigl(\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)\qquad\text{for every }\eta\in\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\Bigr),

and there exists a bounded extension operator
E:H^{1}(D)\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Therefore, up to a multiplicative constant depending only on
D
, we have

(1.77)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}&=\|\mathbf{1}_{D}E(w(0))\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|E(w(0))\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|E(w(0))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1-\eta}\,\|E(w(0))\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\eta}\\
&\lesssim\|w(0)\|_{L^{2}(D)}^{1-\eta}\,\|w(0)\|_{H^{1}(D)}^{\eta}\lesssim\|w(0)\|_{L^{2}(D)}+\|w(0)\|_{L^{2}(D)}^{1-\eta}\|\nabla w(0)\|_{L^{2}(D)}^{\eta}\\
&\lesssim\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1-\eta}\|\nabla w(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\eta}.\end{aligned}

Arguing as for the linearized system (
1.58
), one obtains the parabolic estimate

(1.78)

\displaystyle t^{-1}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla w(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Inserting these bounds into (
1.77
) we obtain the following

(1.79)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,w(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim(t+t^{1-\eta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho(s)}\,u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Plugging this estimate into (
1.75
) yields

(1.80)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim(t+t^{1-\eta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We have finally obtained (see (
1.73
)), after replacing
2t
by
t
, that

(1.81)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\left(t^{-1}+t^{-1-\frac{\eta}{2}}\right)\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Finally, for
t\leq 1
this provides the desired decay in (
1.65
). However, for large times one has the decay
\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sim t^{-1}
, which is not integrable. As a consequence, the time-splitting argument can only be applied on bounded time intervals, and we obtain

(1.82)

\displaystyle\nabla u\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

To overcome this obstruction, inspired by the Galilean transformation, we introduce the momentum per unit mass associated with
u_{j}
, which is conserved in time:

(1.83)

\displaystyle M_{j}=\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,u_{0,j}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{|D_{t}|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t,x)\,u_{j}(t,x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

We then consider the following Galilean change of variables:

(1.84)

\displaystyle\rho_{M_{j}}(t,x):=\rho(t,x+M_{j}t),\qquad u_{j,M_{j}}(t,x):=u_{j}(t,x+M_{j}t)-M_{j},\qquad\bar{w}_{0}(x):=w(0,x)+tM_{j}.

Integrating in space the momentum equation in (
1.74
), we obtain

(1.85)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,w(0,x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(s,x)\,u_{j}(s,x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}s=t\,M_{j}\,|D|.

As a consequence,
\bar{w}_{0}
has zero average on
D
. Moreover, subtracting
t|D||M_{j}|^{2}
from both sides of (
1.75
), we obtain

(1.86)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho_{M_{j}}}\,u_{j,M_{j}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,u_{0,j}(x)\cdot\bar{w}_{0}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Now, since
\bar{w}_{0}
is mean free on
D
, we have
\|\bar{w}_{0}\|_{H^{1}(D)}\lesssim\|\nabla\bar{w}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(D)}
by the Poincaré inequality. For this reason, (
1.77
) can be simplified to

(1.87)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,\bar{w}_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,\bar{w}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1-\eta}\|\nabla\bar{w}_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\eta}.

In the same spirit as before one can improve (
1.80
) to

(1.88)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{\rho_{M_{j}}}\,u_{j,M_{j}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

This shows that, in the moving frame associated with
M_{j}
, the decay is compatible with the desired one and is faster than in the original variables. To conclude, it is enough to observe that the gradient is invariant under translations, and therefore the left-hand side of (
1.73
) is unchanged. Hence for al
t>0
,

(1.89)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(2t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim t^{-1-\frac{\eta}{2}}\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

This is precisely the desired decay estimate.

Therefore, the lack of decay in the original variables is entirely due to a rigid motion component, and does not affect the Lipschitz continuity of the velocity field.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

In Section 2, we establish
a priori
bounds for the functionals
A_{i}^{\eta}
under
L^{2}
initial data and non-negative bounded density.

In Section 3, we investigate the properties of immediately strong solutions. In particular, we prove the
\sqrt{t}
-rate of propagation of the support (point (iv) of
Theorem

1.4
) and the conservation of momentum (point (iii) of
Theorem

1.4
). Moreover, we introduce and discuss a dynamic Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality.

In Section 4, we first prove existence for (
1.2
) via a lifting procedure, and then establish existence by gluing, thereby proving
Theorem

1.4
. We also study the linearized system (
1.58
) and the backward system (
1.74
), establishing both existence and suitable bounds.

In Section 5, we introduce the Galilean transform and show how to obtain global-in-time Lipschitz solutions. We further prove uniqueness and stability within this class, thereby completing the proof of
Theorem

1.5
.

In Section 6, we address more regular patches and analyze the long-time dynamics of Lipschitz patches, proving
Theorem

1.6
and
Theorem

1.7
.

Finally, in the appendix, we discuss the derivation of relative energy identities and characterize the admissible class of test functions for the weak formulation of (
1.2
).

2.
A priori estimates

In this section, we study linear systems of the form

(2.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\dive(\rho u)=0,\\
\rho\partial_{t}v+\rho(u\cdot\nabla)v+\nabla P=\Delta v,\\
\dive v=0,\\
v|_{t=0}=v_{0},\end{cases}

where
v_{0}\in L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
(\rho,u)
if fixed.
Definition

1.3
extends to the system (
2.1
).

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be a weak solution of (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying (
1.16
). We say that
v
is an
immediately strong solution
of (
2.1
), advected by
(\rho,u)
, if the following properties hold:

•

v
is a distributional solution of (
2.1
), in the sense of

Definition

1.2
.

•

The energy inequality holds.

•

There exists a constant
C_{(\rho,u)}(v)
such that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,v,u)\leq C_{(\rho,u)}(v).

In the following we assume that
(\rho,u)
is an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) with respect to initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying

(2.3)

\displaystyle 0<{\varepsilon}\leq\rho_{0}\leq 1,\quad u_{0}\in L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Our goal is to obtain
a priori
estimates on
v
that are independent of
\varepsilon>0
. More precisely, we aim to derive bounds of the form

(2.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,v,u)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Here,
C\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
denotes a continuous and monotonically increasing function. Consequently, by setting
v=u
, we obtain that

(2.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
and
v_{0}
are smooth and therefore
(\rho,u)
and
v
are smooth solution of (
1.2
) and (
2.1
), respectively.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be a solution of (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying (
2.3
), and let
v
be a solution of (
2.1
). Then there exists a constant

(2.6)

\displaystyle C_{u}=C(\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}),

which is non decreasing and independent of
{\varepsilon}>0
, such that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,v,u)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{s}(t,v,u)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}\,\|v_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

In particular, if
v=u
, then

(2.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u)\leq C_{u}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Remark 2.3
.

Notice that, in order to prove (
2.8
), it is sufficient to establish (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) in the case
s=1
. Indeed, interpolating these two estimates yields the result for any
s\in(0,1)
. We therefore present only the proof of (
2.7
), since the estimate (
2.8
) with
s=1
follows in the same way, up to a straightforward modification of the time weights. We refer, for instance, to
[
15
]
.

The proof of (
2.7
) and (
2.9
) will be carried out in the next subsections simultaneously for each
A_{i}
.

2.1.
Proof of
Theorem

2.2

2.1.1.
Estimates for
A_{0}

It is well-known that smooth solutions of (
1.2
) and (
2.1
) satisfy the energy equality and this immediately gives

(2.10)

\displaystyle\sup_{t>0}A^{0}_{0}(t,v,u)\leq\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

2.1.2.
Estimates for
A_{1}

First, notice that by standard Stokes-type estimates, since
\rho
is bounded, we have

(2.11)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}v\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla P\|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{S}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

We test the momentum equation in (
2.1
) with
\dot{v}
and obtain

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\left\langle\Delta v-\nabla P,\dot{v}\right\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\left\langle\nabla v,\nabla(u\cdot\nabla v)\right\rangle+\left\langle P,\dive\dot{v}\right\rangle.

Integration by parts and incompressibility yield

(2.13)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\nabla v,\nabla(u\cdot\nabla)v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle u,\nabla|\nabla v|^{2}\right\rangle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla v\nabla u(\nabla v)^{T})\,\mathrm{d}x\eqqcolon 0+L(t)

Using Gagliardo–Nirenberg, the Stokes estimates (
2.11
), and Young’s inequality, we obtain

(2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |L(t)|&\leq C_{GN}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla^{2}v\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\leq C_{GN}C_{S}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\,\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\end{aligned}

where
C
depends only on
C_{GN}
and
C_{S}
.

For the pressure term, we first use
Proposition

2.9
and then proceed exactly as for
L
:

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\langle P,\dive\dot{v}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}\left\lVert\nabla P\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Combining the above estimates with (
2.12
) and absorbing
\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}
into the left-hand side yields

(2.16)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}.

To prove (
2.7
) for
i=1
, we multiply (
2.16
) by
t
:

(2.17)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(t\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\right)+t\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}+t\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}.

Applying Grönwall’s inequality and using the energy inequality for
(\rho,u)
and
v
leads to

(2.18)

\displaystyle t\|\nabla v(t)\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v(s)\|_{2}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(s)\|_{2}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\lesssim\exp\left(2\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Integrating in time (
2.17
) and using the previous estimate, we see that

(2.19)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}s\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.20)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\exp\left(2\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Setting

(2.21)

\displaystyle C^{0}_{u}\coloneqq\exp\left(4\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right),

and using the Stokes estimates (
2.11
) we conclude

(2.22)

\displaystyle t\|\nabla v(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s\left\lVert\nabla P(s),\nabla^{2}v(s),\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}(s)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim C^{0}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
C^{0}_{u}
is non-decreasing in
\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0}\|_{2}
,
(
2.22
) yields (
2.7
) in the case
i=1
. Moreover, taking
v=u
we obtain (
2.9
) in the case
i=1
.

2.1.3.
Estimates for
A_{2}

To estimate
A_{2}
, it is necessary to differentiate (
1.2
) and (
2.1
) with respect to the material derivative
D
. Here, for every smooth vector field
w\colon[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the material derivative of
w
is given by

(2.23)

\displaystyle Dw=D_{u}w=\partial_{t}w+(u\cdot\nabla)w.

It is clear that
D
does not commute with
\nabla
,
\dive
, and
\Delta
. However, we have the following identities:

(2.24)

\displaystyle D(\nabla w)

\displaystyle=\nabla\dot{w}-\nabla w\,\nabla u,

(2.25)

\displaystyle D(\dive w)

\displaystyle=\dive\dot{w}-\tr(\nabla u\,\nabla w),

(2.26)

\displaystyle D(\Delta w)

\displaystyle=\Delta\dot{w}-\partial_{k}((\partial_{k}u\cdot\nabla)w)-(\partial_{k}u\cdot\nabla)\partial_{k}w.

Note that, since
\rho
satisfies the transport equation with transport field
u
, we have
D_{u}(\rho\dot{v})=\rho\ddot{v}
.

We now turn to the estimate. By applying
D=D_{u}
to (
2.1
), multiplying the result by
\dot{v}
, and integrating, we deduce that

(2.27)

\displaystyle\langle\rho\ddot{v},\dot{v}\rangle=\langle D\Delta v-D\nabla P,\dot{v}\rangle.

The left-hand side simplifies as follows:

(2.28)

\displaystyle\langle\rho\ddot{v},\dot{v}\rangle=\langle\rho[\partial_{t}\dot{v}+(u\cdot\nabla)\dot{v}],\dot{v}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int\rho\partial_{t}|\dot{v}|^{2}+\rho u\cdot\nabla|\dot{v}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int\rho\partial_{t}|\dot{v}|^{2}-\dive(\rho u)|\dot{v}|^{2}.

Using that
-\dive(\rho u)=\partial_{t}\rho
and the product rule for the time derivative, we obtain

(2.29)

\displaystyle\langle\rho\ddot{v},\dot{v}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int\rho\partial_{t}|\dot{v}|^{2}+\partial_{t}\rho|\dot{v}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

To estimate the right-hand side of (
2.27
), we treat the Laplacian and pressure terms separately.

For the Laplacian term, we use identity (
2.26
) to commute the material derivative with the Laplacian and then integrate by parts:

(2.30)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle D\Delta v,\dot{v}\rangle&=\left\langle\Delta\dot{v},\dot{v}\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{k}(\partial_{k}u_{j}\partial_{j}v_{i}),\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{k}u_{j}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}v_{i},\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\left\langle\partial_{k}u_{j}\partial_{j}v_{i},\partial_{k}\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial_{k}u_{j}\partial_{k}v_{i},\partial_{j}\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla v\nabla u(\nabla\dot{v})^{T})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla\dot{v}\nabla u(\nabla v)^{T})\eqqcolon-\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+L(t).\end{aligned}

For the pressure term, we apply Leibniz’s rule in time and integrate by parts:

(2.31)

\displaystyle\langle D\nabla P,\dot{v}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle\nabla P,\dot{v}\rangle-\langle\nabla P,\ddot{v}\rangle\eqqcolon-\frac{d}{dt}\langle P,\dive\dot{v}\rangle+Q(t).

Collecting all terms, we obtain

(2.32)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\frac{d}{dt}\left(\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-\langle P,\dive\dot{v}\rangle\right)+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=L(t)+Q(t).

We now estimate each term on the right-hand side of (
2.32
). To this end, we introduce

(2.33)

\displaystyle C^{1}_{u}\coloneqq\exp\left(9\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right).

For the laplacian term
L(t)
, with the same combination of (
2.14
):

(2.34)

\displaystyle\left\lvert L(t)\right\rvert\leq\underbrace{C\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla^{2}v\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon L_{1}(t)}+\frac{1}{8}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

Thanks to (
2.22
), we obtain:

(2.35)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\left\lvert L_{1}(s)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{s\in(0,t)}\sqrt{s}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}\sup_{s\in(0,t)}\sqrt{s}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s\left\lVert\nabla^{2}v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s\left\lVert\nabla^{2}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.36)

\displaystyle\lesssim(C^{0}_{u})^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

In order to estimate the pressure term
Q
, we use
Proposition

2.9
and Young’s inequality:

(2.37)

\displaystyle Q(t)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{C\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon Q_{1}(t)}+\frac{1}{8}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|^{2}_{2}+\underbrace{C\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon Q_{2}(t)}.

Q_{1}(t)
behaves as
L_{1}(t)
:

(2.38)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}Q_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Inserting (
2.34
) and (
2.37
) in (
2.32
), we obtain

(2.39)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(t)+\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim L_{1}(t)+Q_{1}(t)+Q_{2}(t).

Multiplying (
2.39
) by
s^{2}
and integrating in time, thanks to the estimates on
L_{1}
and
Q_{1}
we get

(2.40)

\displaystyle t^{2}\phi(t)+\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}s\phi(s)\,ds+C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}Q_{2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Notice that, using (
2.15
), we obtain an upper bound for
\phi(t)
:

(2.41)

\displaystyle\phi(t)=\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-\langle P,\dive\dot{v}\rangle\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{2}\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{2}.

Hence, by (
2.22
),

(2.42)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s\phi(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim C^{0}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+C^{0}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C^{1}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

Again by (
2.22
), we obtain a lower bound for
\phi
:

(2.43)

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle\geq\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-C\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}\gtrsim\frac{3}{4}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-C\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}

Then, by (
2.22
),

(2.44)

\displaystyle t^{2}\phi(t)

\displaystyle\gtrsim t^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-(C^{0}_{u})^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq t^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

By inserting (
2.42
) and (
2.44
) into (
2.40
), we obtain

(2.45)

\displaystyle t^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By setting
u=v
in (
2.45
) and applying Young’s inequality, we get

(2.46)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq CC^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla P\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.47)

\displaystyle\leq 2CC^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

and therefore

(2.48)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

By inserting the latter estimate into (
2.45
), we deduce that

(2.49)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla P\|^{2}_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|^{2}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left((C^{0}_{u})^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{aligned}

which, thanks to Stokes estimates (
2.11
), completes the proof of (
2.7
) for
i=2
.

Recall that by
Proposition

2.11

(2.50)

\displaystyle\|\nabla v(t)\|^{2}_{\infty}\lesssim\|\nabla^{2}v(t)\|^{2}_{2}+\|\nabla v(t)\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{2}.

By combining (
2.50
) with (
2.49
), we conclude by Hölder’s inequality

(2.51)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{t}s\,\|\nabla v\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq&\int_{0}^{t}s\,\|\nabla^{2}v\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\,\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim&C_{u}^{0}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+(C^{1}_{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim C_{u}^{1}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Since the right-hand side does not depend on
t
, we conclude

(2.52)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\|\nabla v\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim C_{u}^{1}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

With the same arguments as above, we get

(2.53)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim&C_{u}^{1}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

2.1.4.
Estimates for
A_{3}

Similarly to what was done in the estimate for
A_{2}
, we commute the material derivative with the differential operators following (
2.24
) and (
2.26
) to obtain:

(2.54)

\displaystyle\rho\ddot{v}+\nabla\dot{P}-\Delta\dot{v}=\nabla P\,\nabla u-\partial_{k}((\partial_{k}u\cdot\nabla)v)-(\partial_{k}u\cdot\nabla)\partial_{k}v\eqqcolon F.

Using
Lemma

2.10
, we deduce:

(2.55)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v},\nabla\dot{P}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\rho\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla\dive\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

(2.56)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\rho\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\left(\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla P\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

(2.57)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C\left(\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right).

Here, we have used (
2.11
) in the last inequality. Then by (
2.49
), (
2.52
) and (
2.53
), we deduce that

(2.58)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.59)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{s\in(0,t)}\left(s^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\int_{0}^{t}s\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\sup_{s\in(0,t)}\left(s^{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\int_{0}^{t}s\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.60)

\displaystyle\lesssim(C^{1}_{u})^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Introducing

(2.61)

\displaystyle C^{2}_{u}\coloneqq\exp\left(19\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

we conclude that

(2.62)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v},\nabla\dot{P}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lVert\rho\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+CC^{2}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Now we estimate
A_{3}
. By testing (
2.54
) with
\ddot{v}
, we get

(2.63)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\langle D\nabla P,\ddot{v}\right\rangle=\left\langle D\Delta v,\ddot{v}\right\rangle.

For the term involving the Laplacian, similarly to (
2.30
)

(2.64)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\langle D\Delta v,\ddot{v}\right\rangle=&\left\langle\Delta\dot{v},\partial_{t}\dot{v}\right\rangle+\left\langle\Delta\dot{v},u\cdot\nabla\dot{v}\right\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla v\nabla u(\nabla\ddot{v})^{T})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla\ddot{v}\nabla u(\nabla v)^{T})\\
=&-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|^{2}_{2}+\left\langle\Delta\dot{v},u\cdot\nabla\dot{v}\right\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla v\nabla u(\nabla\ddot{v})^{T})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla\ddot{v}\nabla u(\nabla v)^{T})\\
=:&-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|^{2}_{2}+L_{1}(t)+L_{2}(t)+L_{3}(t).\end{aligned}

For
L_{1}
, we integrate by parts and use the incompressibility

(2.65)

\displaystyle L_{1}(t)=

\displaystyle-\left\langle u_{j}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\dot{v}_{i},\partial_{k}\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{k}u_{j}\partial_{j}\dot{v}_{i},\partial_{k}\dot{v}_{i}\right\rangle=0-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla\dot{v}\nabla u(\nabla\dot{v})^{T}).

Using (
2.62
) we deduce that

(2.66)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lvert L_{1}(s)\right\rvert\leq&\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{4}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\\
\leq&CC^{0}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}C^{1}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lVert\rho\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+CC^{2}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\leq&CC^{2}_{u}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lVert\rho\ddot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

For
L_{2},
we integrate by parts

(2.67)

\displaystyle L_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int\tr(\nabla v\nabla uD(\nabla^{T}\dot{v}))+\int\tr(\nabla v\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}^{T}\nabla u)

(2.68)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\int\tr(\nabla v\nabla u\nabla^{T}\dot{v})\underbrace{-\int\tr(D(\nabla v\nabla u)\nabla^{T}\dot{v})+\int\tr(\nabla v\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}^{T}\nabla u)}_{L_{21}(t)}.

We use the product rule and (
2.24
) and obtain

(2.69)

\displaystyle\left\lVert D(\nabla v\nabla u)\right\rVert_{4/\penalty 3}\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla uD\nabla v\right\rVert_{4/\penalty 3}+\left\lVert\nabla vD\nabla u\right\rVert_{4/\penalty 3}\leq\left\lVert D\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{4}+\left\lVert D\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}

(2.70)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{4}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{u}\right\rVert_{2}.

Thus, we can estimate

(2.71)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{21}(t)\leq&\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert D(\nabla v\nabla u)\right\rVert_{4/\penalty 3}+\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{4}\\
\lesssim&\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{4}\left(\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{4}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{u}\right\rVert_{2}\right).\end{aligned}

By exploiting the same argument as in (
2.66
), we infer that

(2.72)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lvert L_{21}(s)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+CC_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

Similarly, we have:

(2.73)

\displaystyle L_{3}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\int\tr(\nabla u\,\nabla\dot{v}\,\nabla^{T}v)+L_{31}(t),

where
L_{31}
satisfies

(2.74)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left\lvert L_{31}(s)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|^{2}_{2}+CC_{u}^{2}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}.

To simplify the pressure term we integrate by parts

(2.75)

\displaystyle\left\langle D\nabla P,\ddot{v}\right\rangle=\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle\nabla P,\ddot{v}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla P,\dddot{v}\right\rangle=\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle\nabla P,\ddot{v}\right\rangle+\left\langle P,\dive\dddot{v}\right\rangle.

Then, by
Proposition

2.9
,

(2.76)

\displaystyle\left\langle P,\dive\dddot{v}\right\rangle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle P,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle+\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}+\|\nabla\dot{P}\|_{2}\left(\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}+\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}\right)

(2.77)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle P,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle+Q_{1}(t).

For
Q_{1},
similarly to (
2.66
), we have

(2.78)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}|Q_{1}(s)|\,ds\leq{}

\displaystyle\sup_{s>0}\bigl(s\|\nabla P\|_{2}\bigr)\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds\right)^{1/2}

(2.79)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla\dot{P}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+\sup_{s>0}\bigl(s\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+\sup_{s>0}\bigl(s\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds

(2.80)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle CC_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds.

Now we define

(2.81)

\displaystyle\psi(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla v\nabla u\nabla^{T}\dot{v})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tr(\nabla u\,\nabla\dot{v}\,\nabla^{T}v)+\left\langle P,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle+\left\langle\nabla P,\ddot{v}\right\rangle.

Finally, we may write

(2.82)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\psi^{\prime}(t)\leq L_{21}(t)+L_{31}(t)+Q_{1}(t).

Multiplying by
s^{3}
, integrating in time and using the previous estimates

(2.83)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+t^{3}\psi(t)\leq&C\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\psi(s)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\left(L_{21}(s)+L_{31}(s)+Q_{1}(s)\right)\,ds\\
\leq&C\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\psi(s)ds+CC_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds,\end{aligned}

absorbing we get

(2.84)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+t^{3}\psi(t)\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\psi(s)ds+C_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\end{aligned}

Next, we estimate
\psi(t)
from above and below. For the upper bound, we combine Young’s inequality, Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality, and (
2.37
) for the pressure term to obtain

(2.85)

\displaystyle\psi(t)\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}+\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}+\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}\|\nabla u\|_{4}\|\nabla v\|_{4}.

Arguing as in (
2.49
), we infer that

(2.86)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\psi(s)\,ds\lesssim C^{1}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

For the lower bound, we use Young’s inequality:

(2.87)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \psi(t)&\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-C\left\lVert\nabla P\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}-C\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}-C\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{4}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{4}\\
&\geq\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-C\left\lVert\nabla P\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,-C\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}-C\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{4}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert^{2}_{4},\end{aligned}

and so

(2.88)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t^{3}\psi(t)&\gtrsim t^{3}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-t^{3}\left(\left\lVert\nabla P\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,+\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert^{2}_{4}\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert^{2}_{4}\right)-t^{3}\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\\
&\gtrsim t^{3}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-C^{2}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}-t^{3}\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\end{aligned}

Inserting (
2.86
) and (
2.88
) in (
2.84
) we get

(2.89)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+t^{3}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\lesssim t^{3}\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}+C_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Assuming
v=u
, we may apply Young’s inequality to (
2.89
) and obtain

(2.90)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t^{3}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\leq{}&C\,t^{2}\|\nabla P\|_{2}^{2}\,t\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}t^{3}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}+CC_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Absorbing the second term on the right-hand side and using the previous estimates for
\nabla u
and
\nabla P
, we obtain

(2.91)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t^{3}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim C_{u}^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Finally, we may use (
2.91
) to conclude the estimate for (
2.89
). Indeed,

(2.92)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t^{3}\|\nabla P\|_{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}&\lesssim(t\|\nabla P\|_{2})\,(\sqrt{t}\|\nabla v\|_{2})\,(t^{3/2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2})\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{C^{1}_{u}}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}\;\sqrt{C^{0}_{u}}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}\sqrt{C^{2}_{u}}\;\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{2}\lesssim C^{2}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Inserting (
2.92
) into (
2.89
) and using (
2.62
), we obtain

(2.93)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{v},\nabla^{2}\dot{v},\nabla\dot{P}\|_{2}^{2}\,ds+t^{3}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim C^{2}_{u}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}v_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

This concludes the proof of (
2.7
) and (
2.9
) for
i=3
.

2.2.
Auxiliary Results

2.2.1.
Some useful identities

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume that
v,u\colon[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
are smooth divergence-free vector fields. Then the following identities hold:

(2.94)

\displaystyle\dive\dot{v}
