\displaystyle=\tr(\nabla u\nabla v)

(2.95)

\displaystyle\dive\ddot{v}

\displaystyle=2\tr(\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})+\tr(\nabla\dot{u}\nabla v)

(2.96)

\displaystyle\dive\dddot{v}

\displaystyle=D(\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u}))-\tr(\nabla\dot{u}\nabla\dot{v})+\tr(\nabla\dot{u})\tr(\nabla\dot{v})

where we have used the notation
\dot{v}=D_{u}v
.

Proof.

By incompressibility we compute:

(2.97)

\displaystyle\dive\dot{v}=\dive[(u\cdot\nabla)v]=\partial_{i}v_{k}\partial_{k}v_{i}=\tr(\nabla u\nabla v),

which proves (
2.94
).

For (
2.95
), we apply (
2.25
) to interchange
D
and
\dive
:

(2.98)

\displaystyle\dive\ddot{v}=D(\dive\dot{v})+\tr(\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}).

Now, using the product rule and (
2.24
), we compute:

(2.99)

\displaystyle D(\dive\dot{v})

\displaystyle=D\tr(\nabla u\nabla v)

(2.100)

\displaystyle=\tr(D(\nabla u)\nabla v)+\tr(\nabla uD(\nabla v))

(2.101)

\displaystyle=\tr(\nabla\dot{u}\nabla v)-2\tr(\nabla u\nabla u\nabla v)+\tr(\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})

Since both
\nabla u
and
\nabla v
are trace-free
2\times 2
matrices, we recall:

(2.102)

\operatorname{adj}(\nabla u)=-\nabla u\quad\Rightarrow\quad\nabla u\nabla u=-\det(\nabla u)\operatorname{Id}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\tr(\nabla u\nabla u\nabla v)=0

and thus we obtain (
2.95
).

For the third derivative, apply again (
2.25
) and (
2.24
):

(2.103)

\displaystyle\dive\dddot{v}

\displaystyle=D(\dive\ddot{v})+\tr(\nabla u\nabla\ddot{v}).

For the second term, apply the product rule:

(2.104)

\displaystyle\tr(\nabla u\nabla\ddot{v})

\displaystyle=\tr(\nabla uD(\nabla\dot{v}))+\tr(\nabla u\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})

(2.105)

\displaystyle=D\tr(\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})-\tr(D(\nabla u)\nabla\dot{v})+\tr(\nabla u\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})

(2.106)

\displaystyle=D\tr(\nabla u\nabla\dot{v})-\tr(\nabla\dot{u}\nabla\dot{v})+2\tr(\nabla u\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}).

To handle the last term, recall that, due to (
2.102
),

(2.107)

\displaystyle\tr(\nabla\dot{u})=\dive\dot{u}=\tr(\nabla u\nabla u)

(from (
2.94
)). Thus, we have:

(2.108)

\displaystyle 2\tr(\nabla\dot{v}\nabla u\nabla u)=-2\det(\nabla\dot{u})\,\tr(\nabla\dot{v})=-2\tr\big(\nabla\dot{v}\det(\nabla\dot{u})\operatorname{Id}\big)=\tr(\nabla\dot{v})\tr(\nabla\dot{u})

which completes the proof.
∎

2.2.2.
Hardy and BMO

Functions with bounded mean oscillations.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the family of all balls in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A locally integrable function
f
belongs to
\mathrm{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

(2.109)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathrm{BMO}}\;=\;\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}\fint_{B}\bigl|f(x)-\fint_{B}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\bigr|\,\mathrm{d}x\;<\;\infty.

Identifying functions that differ by constants,
\mathrm{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
becomes a Banach space with the norm above.

In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, Poincaré’s inequality on balls yields, for every
B=B(x_{0},R)
and all
f\in H^{1}(B)
,

(2.110)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\,f-\fint_{B}f\bigr\|_{L^{2}(B)}\;\leq\;C\,R\;\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(B)},

whence, by Hölder’s inequality,

(2.111)

\displaystyle\fint_{B}\bigl|f(x)-\fint_{B}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\bigr|\,\mathrm{d}x\;\leq\;\frac{C}{R}\bigl\|\,f-\fint_{B}f\bigr\|_{L^{2}(B)}.

Together, these estimates imply the following:

Lemma 2.5
.

There exists
C>0
such that for every
f\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\nabla f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(2.112)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathrm{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\;\leq\;C\,\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Hardy Space.

The
Hardy space

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a subspace of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consisting of all functions
f\in L^{1}
whose Riesz transforms also belong to
L^{1}
:

(2.113)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):R_{j}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ 1\leq j\leq d\right\},

where
R_{j}=\partial_{j}(-\Delta)^{1/2}
.
For the norm, alternative definitions, and equivalence between them, we refer to
[
17
]
.

We recall the following famous result.

Theorem 2.6

(Fefferman–Stein)
.

The dual of the Hardy space
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with equivalent norms.

We also need the following important result, see
[
5
]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
v,z\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
be two vector fields such that
\dive v=0
and
\operatorname{curl}z=0
. Then, we have that
v\cdot z\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the estimate

(2.114)

\displaystyle\|u\cdot v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where
C
is a constant independent of
u
and
v.

Theorem

2.6
and
Theorem

2.7
allow us to show more accurate estimates of certain products which cannot be deduced from Hölder’s inequality. We obtain the following.

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
v,z\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
be two vector fields such that
\dive v=0
and
\operatorname{curl}z=0
and let
\varphi\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Then we have the estimate

(2.115)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\,v(x)\cdot z(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Moreover, if
v\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then we have
\det(\nabla v)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

(2.116)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(x)\det(\nabla v(x))\,\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Proof.

Since
v\cdot z\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by
Theorem

2.7
and since
\varphi\in\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by
Lemma

2.5
, the estimate in (
2.115
) follows from
Theorem

2.6
. The estimate in (
2.116
) follows from the identity

(2.117)

\displaystyle\det(\nabla\varphi)=\nabla^{\perp}\varphi_{1}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}

and from (
2.115
).
∎

Combining (
2.115
), (
2.116
) and
Lemma

2.4
, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 2.9
.

Assume
v,u:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
are sufficiently regular divergence-free vector fields. Then, for every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

(2.118)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\,\dive\dot{v}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2},

(2.119)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\,\dive\ddot{v}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{2}\left(\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\nabla\dot{u}\right\rVert_{2}\right),

(2.120)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\dive\dddot{v}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle\varphi,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle+\|\nabla\varphi\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}

(2.121)

\displaystyle+\|\nabla\dot{\varphi}\|_{2}\left(\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}+\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}\right)

Proof.

The first two estimates follow by combining (
2.94
) and (
2.95
) with (
2.115
), and observing that

(2.122)

\displaystyle\tr(\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})=\partial_{i}v_{j}\,\partial_{j}\dot{u}_{i}=\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\dot{u}_{i},

where
\dive(\partial_{i}v)=0
(due to divergence-free condition) and
\curl(\nabla u_{i})=0
, with similar reasoning applying to
\tr(\nabla v\nabla u)
and
\tr(\nabla\dot{v}\nabla u)
.

With (
2.96
), we can write

(2.123)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\dive\dddot{v}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi D(\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u}))

(2.124)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi\det(\nabla(\dot{v}+\dot{u}))-\det(\nabla\dot{v})-\det(\nabla\dot{u})\eqqcolon I(t)+II(t).

For
I
, observe that

(2.125)

\displaystyle I(t)=

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle\varphi,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle-\left\langle\dot{\varphi},\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle

(2.126)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle\varphi,\tr(3\nabla u\nabla\dot{v}+\nabla v\nabla\dot{u})\right\rangle+\|\nabla\dot{\varphi}\|_{2}\left(\|\nabla v\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}+\|\nabla u\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2}\right).

Note that we used (
2.122
) to estimate the second term in the last step. In order to use estimate
II
, we use the following identity

(2.127)

\displaystyle\det\big(\nabla(\dot{v}+\dot{u})\big)-\det(\nabla\dot{v})-\det(\nabla\dot{u})=-\nabla\dot{v}_{1}\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\dot{u}_{2}-\nabla\dot{u}_{1}\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\dot{v}_{2}.

Using (
2.115
) and (
2.127
), we can estimate
II
as

(2.128)

\displaystyle II(t)\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}\|_{2},

which completes the proof.
∎

2.2.3.
Linear Stokes estimates

We start with the following estimate for the Stokes operator, see
[
15
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Let
(u,P)
be a solution of the following Stokes equation

(2.129)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\Delta u+\nabla P=f,\\
\dive(u)=g,\\
u\to 0,\quad\left\lvert x\right\rvert\to\infty,\end{cases}

where
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
g\in\dot{W}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Then we have that

(2.130)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u,\nabla P\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\nabla g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We finish this section with an important
L^{\infty}
-estimates for the gradient of the velocity of solutions (
2.1
).
The case
v=u
was shown in
[
22
]
, the extension to the linear case is straightforward.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) and assume that
v
is an immediately strong solution of (
2.1
). Then, for every
t>0,

(2.131)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla v(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1/2}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1/2}.

3.
Properties of immediately strong solutions

3.1.
The continuity equation

In this subsection, we recall some basic properties of bounded solutions to the continuity equation.
Let
T>0
be fixed and let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Let

(3.1)

u\in L^{1}\big((0,T);L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),\qquad\dive u(t,\cdot)=0\quad\text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

We consider the continuity equation on
(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\dive(\rho u)=0,\\
\rho(0,\cdot)=\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D}.\end{cases}

Definition 3.1

(Weak and renormalized solutions)
.

We say that
\rho\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a weak solution of (
3.2
) (under the assumption (
3.1
)) if for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}\,\varphi(0,\cdot)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

We say that
\rho
is a renormalized solution of (
3.2
) if, for every
\beta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, the function
\beta(\rho)
is a weak solution of (
3.2
) with initial datum
\beta(\rho_{0})
.

Under assumption (
3.1
), the continuity equation (
3.2
) admits weak solutions, which are renormalized under additional regularity assumptions on the velocity field.

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume that
u
satisfies (
3.1
). Then there exists a weak solution
\rho
of (
3.2
) with initial datum
\rho_{0}
. If, in addition, for some
q\in[1,\infty)
,

(3.4)

u\in L^{1}\big((0,T);W^{1,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),\qquad\frac{u}{1+|x|}\in L^{1}\big((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})+L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big),

then there exists a unique renormalized solution
\rho\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover, up to a modification on a negligible set of times, one has

(3.5)

\displaystyle\rho\in C_{w^{\ast}}\big([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\rho\in C\big([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\ \text{for every }p\in[1,\infty).

Proof.

The existence of weak solutions is proved in
[
16
, Proposition II.4]
. The time continuity properties are pointed out in
[
31
]
. The uniqueness and renormalization results follow from
[
16
]
.
∎

3.2.
A
-property and
L^{p}
strip estimate

In this subsection we investigate additional properties of weak solutions to the continuity equation (
3.2
) under the assumption (
3.1
), and assuming further regularity on the solution–velocity pair
(\rho,u)
:

(3.6)

\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\,u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}\big((0,T);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)}\eqqcolon M<\infty.

Throughout, the regularity of
D
is crucial. Since
D
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, it enjoys the so-called
(A)
–property.

Definition 3.3
.

A set
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
has the
(A)
–property if there exists a constant
A>0
such that for every
r>0
and every

x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

(3.7)

\displaystyle d:=\operatorname{dist}(x,D)<r,

one has

(3.8)

|B_{r}(x_{0})\cap D|\;\geq\;\min\{A\,(r-d)^{2},\left\lvert D\right\rvert\}.

The
(A)
–property is used to track the size of the mixing zone between mass and vacuum. For this reason for
t>0
and
R>0
a key role is played by the following time-dependent neighbourhood of
D
:

(3.9)

\displaystyle D_{t,R}:=D+B_{R\sqrt{t}}(0).

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
u
satisfy (
3.1
) and let
\rho
be a weak solution of (
3.2
) on
(0,T)
. Assume that the pair
(\rho,u)
satisfies (
3.6
), and fix
R>0
. Then there exists a positive constant

(3.10)

\displaystyle L=L(M,R,T,D),

which is increasing with respect to both
M
and
R
, such that for every
t\in(0,T)
and every
x\in D_{t,R}
one has

(3.11)

\int_{B_{\sqrt{t}\,L}(x)}\rho(t,x)\,dx\;\gtrsim_{T,D}\;t.

Proof.

For simplicity we assume
\left\lvert D\right\rvert=1
. Since
x\in D+B_{R\sqrt{t}}(0)
, we have
d:=\dist(x,D)<R\sqrt{t}
.
By the
(A)
–property, there exists a constant
A>0
, such that for every
r>d

(3.12)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(x)}\rho_{0}(x)\,dx=|B_{r}(x)\cap D|\geq\min\{A\,(r-d)^{2},1\}.

We then set
r:=(R+1)\sqrt{t}
.
Using
d\leq R\sqrt{t}
, we obtain

(3.13)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(x)}\rho_{0}(x)\,dx\geq\min\{At,1\}=C\;t,\qquad\text{with }C:=\min\{A,T^{-1}\}.

Following
the argument of
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
with

c_{0}:=C_{T}\;t
and
R_{0}=r
, we infer that if

(3.14)

\displaystyle Q\geq(R+1)\sqrt{t}+\frac{2t}{\sqrt{C\;t}\,}\,M=:\sqrt{t}\;L

then

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{Q}(x)}\rho(t,x)\,dx\geq\frac{C\;t}{4}.

Since
L
is increasing in both
R
and
M
we conclude the thesis.
∎

Now we show that inside
D_{t,R}
one can control the
L^{p}
norm of a general measurable function
v
in a scale-critical way.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
u
satisfy (
3.1
) and let
\rho
be a weak solution of
(
3.2
) in
(0,T)
. Assume that
(\rho,u)
satisfies (
3.6
), and fix
R>0
.
Then there exist two constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that, for any measurable function
v
,
any
t\in(0,T)
and any
x\in D_{t,R}
, the following holds.

For any
p\in[2,\infty)

(3.16)

t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\,\|v\|_{L^{p}(B_{\sqrt{t}L}(x))}\lesssim_{T,D,p}C_{1}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(B_{\sqrt{t}L}(x))}+\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B_{\sqrt{t}L}(x))}\right)

Moreover, if
p=\infty
, then for any
q>2
we have

(3.17)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{R,t})}\lesssim_{T,D,q}C_{2}\left(t^{1-\frac{1}{q}}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right).

Both constants depend in a non decreasing way on
R
and
M
.

Proof.

We pick
L
as in
Lemma

3.4
and consider
t\in(0,T)
and
x\in D_{R,t}
. For simplicity we use the shortcut
B=B_{\sqrt{t}L}(x)
. We introduce the weighted average

(3.18)

\displaystyle v_{\rho}=\frac{\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,v(y)\,dy}{\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,dy}.

To estimate
v_{\rho}
, we use Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the numerator

(3.19)

\displaystyle\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,|v(y)|\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\;|B|^{1/2}\,\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B)}\lesssim\;Lt^{1/\penalty 2}\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B)}.

and
Lemma

3.4
for the denominator

(3.20)

\displaystyle\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,dy\gtrsim_{T,D}\,t.

Therefore we conclude

(3.21)

\displaystyle\left\lvert v_{\rho}\right\rvert\lesssim_{T,D}L\,t^{-1/2}\,\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B)}.

Let
\bar{v}
be the average of
v
in
B
. By (
3.21
) we can estimate the difference between the weighted average and the average. Indeed, since
\rho\leq 1

(3.22)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\lvert v_{\rho}-\bar{v}\right\rvert&=\left\lvert(v-\bar{v})_{\rho}\right\rvert\lesssim L\,t^{-1/2}\,\left\lVert v-\bar{v}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B)}.\end{aligned}

The last ingredient we need is the Sobolev–Poincaré inequality in dimension two. For
p\in[2,\infty)

(3.23)

\displaystyle\|v-\bar{v}\|_{L^{p}(B)}\lesssim_{p}\,(\sqrt{t}L)^{2/p}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(B)}.

Finally we can estimate
v
in
L^{p}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{p}(B)}\leq\|v-\bar{v}\|_{L^{p}(B)}+|v_{\rho}-\bar{v}||B|^{1/p}+|v_{\rho}||B|^{1/p}.

Using (
3.21
), (
3.22
), (
3.23
) and
|B|\leq 4tL^{2}
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|v\|_{L^{p}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T,,pD}\;L^{\frac{2}{p}}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(B)}+L^{\frac{2}{p}+2}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(B)}+L^{\frac{2}{p}+1}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B)}

and since
L
depends in a non decreasing way on
M
and
R
we conclude (
3.16
). For the case
p=\infty
, we fix
{\varepsilon}>0
and choose
x^{\prime}\in B
such that

(3.26)

\displaystyle|v(x^{\prime})|\leq\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_{y\in B}|v(y)|+\varepsilon.

Then, by (
3.21
), we obtain

(3.27)

\displaystyle|v(x^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,|v(y)|\,dy}{\int_{B}\rho(t,y)\,dy}+\varepsilon\lesssim_{T,D}L\,t^{-1/2}\,\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(B)}.

Then, by Morrey’s inequality for any
q>2
:

(3.28)

\displaystyle|v(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|v(x)-v(x^{\prime})|+|v(x^{\prime})|

(3.29)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T,D,p}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}|x-x^{\prime}|^{1-2/q}+L\,t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\varepsilon

(3.30)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(Lt^{\frac{1}{2}})^{1-2/q}+L\,t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\varepsilon.

Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and using the arbitrariness of
x\in D_{t,R}
, we conclude (
3.17
).
∎

The following corollary will be a key point in the subsequent analysis.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be a Leray–Hopf weak solution arising from (

L
2

-patch
). Then, for any
R>0
and
T>0
, there are two constants
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
such that, for any measurable
function
v
, and every
t\in(0,T)
the following holds:

For any
p\in[2,\infty)
,

(3.31)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\,\|v\|_{L^{p}(D_{R,t})}\lesssim_{T,D,p}C_{1}\Big(t^{1/\penalty 2}\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\Big).

If
p=\infty
, then for any
q>2
,

(3.32)

t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{R,t})}\lesssim_{T,D,q}C_{2}\left(t^{1-\frac{1}{q}}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right)

Both constants depend monotonically on
R
and
\|\rho_{0}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.

Proof.

Set
M=\|\rho_{0}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. By the energy inequality this is an admissible choice in (
3.6
).
The claim follows by combining
Proposition

3.5
with the Besicovitch covering theorem.
∎

3.3.
Finite propagation of the density

We now apply the theory from the previous subsection to immediately strong solutions arising from initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying (

L
2

-patch
), namely

(3.33)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad 0\leq\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D},

where
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain.
Let
(\rho,u)
be a weak solution of (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
, and let
v
be an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
.
We recall that, by
Definition

2.1
, there exists a constant
C_{(\rho,u)}(v)
such that

(3.34)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,v,u)\leq C_{(\rho,u)}(v).

All the properties studied in the next two subsections become particularly transparent in the self-advected case, namely when
v=u
; however, for future analysis we will make essential use of the properties of solutions to the linearized system, which is why we work in this more general setting.

We start by proving estimates on the
L^{\infty}
norm of the solution.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(\rho,u)
a weak solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
) and
v
an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
. Then, for any
R>0
there exist two constants

(3.35)

\displaystyle C=C(R,\left\lVert\rho_{0}u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},C_{(\rho,u)}(v)),\qquad C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\left\lVert\rho_{0}u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},C_{(\rho,u)}(v))

such that for every
T>0

(3.36)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert v(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(D_{t,R})}\lesssim_{T,D}C,\quad\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\left\lVert\dot{v}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(D_{t,R})}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim_{T,D}C

and every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(3.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}t^{3/\penalty 4}\frac{\left\lvert v(t,x)\right\rvert}{(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)}\lesssim_{T,D}C^{\prime},\quad\int_{0}^{T}t^{5/\penalty 2}\frac{\left\lvert\dot{v}(t,x)\right\rvert^{2}}{(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim_{T,D}C^{\prime}.

Both
C
and
C^{\prime}
are non-decreasing in their arguments.

Proof.

Choosing
v=v(t)
,
q=4
in
Corollary

3.6
we have

(3.38)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{R,t})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(t^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.\right)

(3.39)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(t^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla v(t)\|^{1/\penalty 2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\nabla^{2}v(t)\|^{1/\penalty 2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+C_{(\rho,u)}(v)^{1/\penalty 2}\right)

(3.40)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(t^{\frac{3}{4}}C_{(\rho,u)}(v)^{1/\penalty 2}t^{-1/\penalty 4}t^{-1/\penalty 2}+C_{(\rho,u)}(v)^{1/\penalty 2}\right)

and this proves the first estimate in (
3.36
). For the estimate on
\dot{u}
we use
v=\dot{u}(t)
and as before we get

(3.41)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{R,t})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(t^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)\,\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}).

Multiplying both sided by
t^{1/\penalty 2}
, squaring and integerating in
(0,T)
we get

(3.42)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{R,t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{2}}\int_{0}^{T}t^{\frac{5}{2}}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}t\|\rho\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The last term is bounded by
C_{(\rho,u)}(v)
and

(3.43)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{\frac{5}{2}}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{2}}\;\int_{0}^{T}t^{\frac{5}{2}}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}t^{3}\|\nabla^{2}\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,dt.

which is also bounded by the same constant and concludes (
3.36
).

To prove (
3.37
) we consider
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
y\in D
:

(3.44)

\displaystyle\left\lvert v(t,x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert v(t,x)-v(t,y)\right\rvert+\left\lvert v(t,y)\right\rvert

(3.45)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\nabla v(t)\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{1/\penalty 2}+Ct^{-1/\penalty 2}

(3.46)

\displaystyle\leq CC_{(\rho,u)}(v)^{1/\penalty 2}t^{-\frac{3}{4}}\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{1/\penalty 2}+CT^{1/\penalty 2}t^{-3/\penalty 4}

where, in the second inequality, we have used Morrey’s inequality and (
3.36
) for
R=0
. Now, since
y\in D,
we simply estimate

(3.47)

\displaystyle\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{1/\penalty 2}\leq C(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)

where
C
depends on
\operatorname{diam}(D)
and so we get the estimate for
v
in (
3.37
). For
\dot{v}
in the same way we get

(3.48)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\dot{v}(t,x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)+\left\lvert\dot{v}(t,y)\right\rvert

Then, dividing by
1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert
, multiplying by
t^{5/\penalty 4}
, squaring and integrating in
(0,T)
we get

(3.49)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{5/\penalty 2}\frac{\left\lvert\dot{v}(t,x)\right\rvert^{2}}{(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}t^{5/\penalty 2}\left\lVert\nabla\dot{v}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}t^{5/\penalty 2}\left\lVert\dot{v}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(D)}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The right hand side is bounded by (
3.43
) and (
3.36
). This concludes (
3.37
).
∎

We immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
(\rho,u)
and immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
). Then

(3.50)

\displaystyle\frac{u}{1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

In particular,
\rho
is the unique renormalized solution (
3.2
) with vector field
u
and, up to a modification on a negligible set of times, one has

(3.51)

\displaystyle\rho\in C_{w^{\ast}}\big([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\rho\in C\big([0,\infty);L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\ \text{for every }p\in[1,\infty).

Proof.

(
3.50
) follows immediately from (
3.37
) in the case
u=v
. Now, the rest follows from
Proposition

3.2
.
∎

We now show that the support of the density moves with a scale-critical speed. More precisely, the transported patch remains confined in a
\sqrt{t}
–neighbourhood of the initial domain.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(\rho,u)
be an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from
(

L
2

-patch
), and fix
T>0
. Then there exists a constant

(3.52)

\displaystyle C=C\big(\|\rho_{0}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},C_{(\rho,u)}(u),T,D\big)

such that, for almost every
t\in[0,T)
and for almost every
x\in D
,

(3.53)

\displaystyle|X(t,x)-x|\leq C\,t^{1/2}.

In particular,

(3.54)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\rho(t)\subseteq D+B_{C\sqrt{t}}(0).

Proof.

Since
u
satisfies (
3.50
) and enjoys Sobolev regularity provided by the energy inequality, there exists a unique regular Lagrangian flow

(3.55)

\displaystyle X:[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

associated with
u
(see
[
8
]
).
Then, for every
t\in(0,T)
and every
x\in D
, we have

(3.56)

\displaystyle|X(t,x)-x|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|u(s,X(s,x))|\,\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.57)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|u(s,x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}|u(s,x)-u(s,X(s,x))|\,\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.58)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|u(s,x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}s+C_{M}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}|x-X(s,x)|^{1/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s,

where we have used Morrey’s inequality.

For the first term, there exists a constant

(3.59)

\displaystyle C=C\bigl(\|\rho_{0}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},C_{(\rho,u)}(u),D\bigr)

(see (
3.36
) with
v=u
) such that

(3.60)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|u(s,x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|u(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim_{T,D}C\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C\,t^{1/2}.

For the second term, we use the interpolation inequality

(3.61)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1/2}\|\nabla^{2}u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1/2}\leq C_{(\rho,u)}(u)^{1/2}\,s^{-3/4}.

Up to redefining the constant
C
, we conclude that

(3.62)

\displaystyle|X(t,x)-x|\lesssim_{C}t^{1/2}+\int_{0}^{t}s^{-3/4}|x-X(s,x)|^{1/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s.

Using fractional Grönwall’s we get

(3.63)

\displaystyle\left\lvert X(t,x)-x\right\rvert\lesssim_{C}t^{1/2}

which concludes the proof.
∎

We conclude this subsection by establishing a regularity property for the velocity field away from the initial time.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
(\rho,u)
an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
) and
v
an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
. Then

(3.64)

\displaystyle v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big((0,\infty);H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big).

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
, then by (
2.2
) we have

(3.65)

\displaystyle v,\,\dot{v}\in L^{\infty}\!\big((\varepsilon,\infty);\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big).

Fix also
T>{\varepsilon}
and let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact.
We also claim that

(3.66)

\displaystyle v\in L^{\infty}\!\big((\varepsilon,T);L^{\infty}(K)\big),\qquad\dot{v}\in L^{2}\!\big((\varepsilon,T);L^{\infty}(K)\big).

The first inclusion in (
3.66
) follows directly from (
3.37
). For the
second one, we note that for every
x\in K

(3.67)

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{0}|\dot{v}(t,x)|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq_{\varepsilon,K}\int_{\varepsilon}^{T}t^{5/2}\,\frac{|\dot{v}(t,x)|^{2}}{(1+|x|)^{2}}\,dt\leq C^{\prime},

where the last inequality follows again from (
3.37
) and proves (
3.66
).

We now turn to the estimates for
\partial_{t}v
. Recall that

(3.68)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v=\dot{v}-(u\cdot\nabla)v,\qquad\nabla\partial_{t}v=\nabla\dot{v}-\nabla\big[(u\cdot\nabla)v\big].

From the first identity in (
3.68
), we obtain

(3.69)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}v(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}+\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\,\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which belongs to
L^{2}(\varepsilon,T)
by (
3.66
) and
(
3.65
) applied to both
v
and
u
. In a similar way,

(3.70)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\partial_{t}v(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\nabla[(u\cdot\nabla)v](t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}.

The first term belongs to
L^{\infty}(\varepsilon,T)
by (
3.65
).
For the second one, we estimate

(3.71)

\displaystyle\|\nabla[(u\cdot\nabla)v]\|_{L^{2}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\,\|\nabla^{2}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

which belongs to
L^{\infty}(\varepsilon,T)
by (
1.24
), (
2.2
) and
(
3.66
). Since the divergence-free condition is propagated in time, the claim
follows.
∎

3.4.
Conservation of momentum and tail estimates

Fix
T>0
, thanks to
Proposition

3.9
, the density remains compactly supported in
space–time. More precisely there exists a compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
(possibly depending on
T
), such that for all

t\in[0,T)
,

(3.72)

\operatorname{supp}\rho(t)\subseteq K.

This property is sufficient to prove conservation of the total momentum for
immediately strong solutions. We emphasize that, for general initial densities,
momentum conservation in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is still unknown.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(\rho,u)
an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
) and
v
an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
. Then, for almost every
t>0
,

(3.73)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t,x)\,v(t,x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,v_{0}(x)\,dx.

Moreover,

(3.74)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t,x)\,\dot{v}(t,x)\,dx=0,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|\,|\rho(t,x)\,\dot{u}(t,x)|\,dx\lesssim_{K}\|\rho\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where
K
is defined in (
3.72
).

Proof.

Fix
T>0.
Since
v
is an immediately strong solution of (
2.1
), it satisfies the weak
formulation of the momentum equation in the sense of
Definition

1.2
. By a
standard time–cutoff argument, for every

\varphi\in C_{c,\sigma}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and for a.e.
t>0
,

(3.75)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)\,v(t)\cdot\varphi-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}\,v_{0}\cdot\varphi=-\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,u\otimes v:\nabla\varphi+\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v:\nabla\varphi.

Let
N>0
and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that
\chi\equiv 1
on

B(0,1)
and
\chi\equiv 0
outside
B(0,2)
. Define

(3.76)

\displaystyle\chi_{N}(x):=\chi\!\left(\frac{x}{N}\right),

so that
\chi_{N}\equiv 1
on
B(0,N)
,
\supp\chi_{N}\subset B(0,2N)
, and

\|\nabla\chi_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim N^{-1}
. Fix
a\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and set

(3.77)

\displaystyle\varphi_{N}(x):=\nabla^{\perp}\!\big((x\cdot a^{\perp})\,\chi_{N}(x)\big).

Then
\dive\varphi_{N}=0
,
\supp\varphi_{N}\subset B(0,2N)
, and

\varphi_{N}(x)\to a
pointwise as
N\to\infty
. Moreover, defining

(3.78)

\displaystyle A_{N}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\ N\leq|x|\leq 2N\},

we have
\supp\nabla\varphi_{N}\subset A_{N}
and

(3.79)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi_{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C|a|,

with
C
independent of
N
.

Using
\varphi_{N}
as a test function in (
3.75
), and
observing that for
N
large enough
\supp\rho(t)\cap A_{N}=\emptyset
, the
convective term vanishes and we obtain

(3.80)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)\,v(t)\cdot\varphi_{N}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}\,v_{0}\cdot\varphi_{N}=\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v:\nabla\varphi_{N}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

(3.81)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v:\nabla\varphi_{N}\right|\leq C|a|\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}((0,t)\times A_{N})},

which converges to zero as
N\to\infty
. Passing to the limit in (
3.80
) yields
(
3.73
). Indeed, the limit is well defined since

\rho v\in L^{\infty}((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, as a consequence of the uniform compact support
\supp\rho(t)\subset K
.

Fix now
\varepsilon>0
and define
v^{n}:=v\,\chi_{n}
. By
Lemma

3.10
,

(3.82)

\displaystyle v^{n}\in H^{1}\bigl((\varepsilon,T);H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),

and
v^{n}
is an admissible test function for the weak formulation of the
transport equation. Therefore, for a.e.
s,t\in(\varepsilon,T)
,

(3.83)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)\,v^{n}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(s)\,v^{n}(s)=\int_{s}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,\partial_{t}v^{n}+\rho\,u\cdot\nabla v^{n}.

The regularity of
v
given by (
3.10
) together with (
3.72
) allows us to
pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in (
3.83
). By
(
3.73
), the left-hand side vanishes, and we obtain

(3.84)

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\,\dot{v}=0\qquad\text{for a.e.\ }s,t\in(\varepsilon,T).

By the freedom in the choice of
{\varepsilon}>0
and
T>0.

we get the first identity in (
3.74
). The second estimate follows
from the compact support of
\rho(t)
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.
∎

The main consequence of
Lemma

3.11
is the derivation of tail estimates for
(u,P)
.
These follow from the properties of the stationary Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

(3.85)

\begin{cases}-\nabla Q+\Delta w=f,\\
\dive w=0.\end{cases}

with mean free source.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfy

(3.86)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)\,dy=0,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|y|\,|f(y)|\,dy\leq\widehat{C}.

Then there exists a distributional solution
(\tilde{w},\tilde{Q})
of (
3.85
)
such that
\nabla\tilde{w}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

(3.87)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\tilde{Q}(x)\right\rvert=\widehat{C}\,O(|x|^{-2}),\qquad\left\lvert\tilde{w}(x)\right\rvert=\widehat{C}\,O(|x|^{-1}),\qquad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.

Moreover, if
(w,Q)
is another distributional solution of (
3.85
)
such that
\nabla w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then there exists a constant vector

c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
w=\tilde{w}+c
and
\nabla Q=\nabla\tilde{Q}

Proof.

The fundamental solution of the two-dimensional stationary Stokes system is given by
(see
[
18
, IV.2.4]
)

(3.88)

q(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\frac{x}{|x|^{2}},\qquad W(x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{Id}}{|x|}+\frac{x\otimes x}{|x|^{2}}\right).

Here
\mathrm{Id}
denotes the identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
.
Convolving with the source term
f
, we define

(3.89)

\tilde{Q}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q(x-y)\cdot f(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad\tilde{w}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W(x-y)\,f(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

Using Taylor expansions, we obtain, as
\left\lvert y\right\rvert\ll|x|
and
\left\lvert x\right\rvert\to\infty
,

(3.90)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} q(x-y)&=O(|x|^{-1})+y\,O(|x|^{-2}),\\
W(x-y)&=O(\log|x|)+y\,O(|x|^{-1}),\\
\nabla W(x-y)&=O(|x|^{-1})+y\,O(|x|^{-2}).\end{aligned}

Therefore,

(3.91)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(x)=O(|x|^{-1})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y+O(|x|^{-2})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot f(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y,

which, by (
3.86
), yields the first estimate in (
3.85
).
The estimate
\tilde{w}(x)=O(|x|^{-1})
follows in the same way.

Again by the previous expansions, we have

\nabla\tilde{w}(x)=O(|x|^{-2})
as
|x|\to\infty
, hence

\nabla\tilde{w}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}(0))
for
R
big enough.
Moreover, by Stokes estimates,

(3.92)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\tilde{Q},\nabla^{2}\tilde{w}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which implies
\nabla\tilde{w}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and therefore

\nabla\tilde{w}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Finally, let
(w,Q)
be another distributional solution of (
3.85
) such that
\nabla w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then
Q-\tilde{Q}
is harmonic and has gradient in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(by the Stokes estimates), hence it is constant.
Since
\nabla Q=\nabla\tilde{Q}
, it follows that
w-\tilde{w}
is harmonic with gradient in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and therefore it is constant.
∎

Collecting the two previous lemma
Lemma

3.11
and
Lemma

3.12
we get the following proposition.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
(\rho,u)
an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
) and
v
an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
. Then, up to redefining the pressure (of (
2.1
)) by a constant, for almost
every
t>0
we have, as
|x|\to\infty
,

(3.93)

\displaystyle|Q(t,x)|

\displaystyle=\|\rho\,\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,O(|x|^{-2}),

(3.94)

\displaystyle|v(t,x)|

\displaystyle=\|\rho\,\dot{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,O(|x|^{-1})+c(t).

Proof.

Set
f(t,x):=(\rho\dot{v})(t,x)
. By
Lemma

3.11
,
in particular by (
3.74
), the conditions (
3.86
) are satisfied
for almost every
t>0
. Hence, there exists a distributional solution

(\tilde{w}(t),\tilde{Q}(t))
of

(3.95)

\begin{cases}-\nabla\tilde{Q}(t)+\Delta\tilde{w}(t)=\rho\dot{v}(t),\\
\dive\tilde{w}(t)=0,\end{cases}

enjoying the decay properties stated in
Lemma

3.12
.

Since
\nabla v(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the uniqueness result in

Lemma

3.12
implies that

(3.96)

\displaystyle v(t)=\tilde{w}(t)+c(t)\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla Q(t)=\nabla\tilde{Q}(t).

Therefore, up to redefining the pressure by an additive constant, we may assume
Q(t)=\tilde{Q}(t)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
(\rho,u)
an immediately strong solution of (
1.2
) arising from (

L
2

-patch
) and
v
an immediately strong solution to (
2.1
) advected by
(\rho,u)
. Let
h\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be divergence free and such that

h(x)=O(1)
as
|x|\to\infty
. Then, for almost every
t>0)
,

(3.97)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\dot{v}(t,x)\cdot h(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v(t,x):\nabla h(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

Let
\chi_{N}
be the cutoff function introduced in the proof of

Lemma

3.11
, and set
h_{N}:=h\,\chi_{N}
, so that
h_{N}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Since for every
\varepsilon>0
we have

(3.98)

\displaystyle\rho\dot{v}+\nabla P=\Delta v\quad\text{in }L^{2}((\varepsilon,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

we may multiply (
3.98
) against
h_{N}
. Integrating by parts and using that

\dive h=0
, we obtain for almost every
t\in(0,\infty)

(3.99)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho\dot{v}(t)\cdot h+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v(t):\nabla h=-\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(t)\,h\cdot\nabla\chi_{N}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v(t)\,(h\otimes\nabla\chi_{N})\Bigg).

By
Proposition

3.13
, for almost every
t>0
and for
|x|\geq N
with big
N
,

(3.100)

\displaystyle|Q(t,x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{2}},\qquad|v(t,x)|\lesssim 1.

Therefore,

(3.101)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(t)\,h\cdot\nabla\chi_{N}

\displaystyle\leq\|Q(t)\,h\|_{L^{1}(A_{N})}\,\frac{1}{N}\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{2}}\,|A_{N}|\,\frac{1}{N}\;\longrightarrow\;0,

and

(3.102)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v(t)\,(h\otimes\nabla\chi_{N})\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{2}(A_{N})}\,\|h\|_{L^{2}(A_{N})}\,\frac{1}{N}\lesssim\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{2}(A_{N})}\;\longrightarrow\;0.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Existence of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
solutions

In this section we prove an existence result for (
1.2
).
Throughout, we assume

(4.1)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{D},\qquad D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ bounded Lipschitz domain},\qquad u_{0}\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

4.1.
Existence by lifting

As described in the introduction, our strategy is to approximate the vacuum density by strictly positive
densities. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

(4.2)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{D}+\frac{1}{n},

and denote by
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
the unique solution of (
1.2
)
with initial data
(\rho_{0}^{n},u_{0})
.

By
Theorem

2.2
, and in particular by (
2.9
), there exists a constant
C_{u}=C(\|\sqrt{\rho^{n}_{0}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})
such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{n})\leq C_{u}\;\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Since

(4.4)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq 2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

and since
C_{u}
is non-decreasing in its argument, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, such that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{n})\leq C.

Thanks to the uniform estimates (
4.5
) we can show compactness properties of the sequence
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
.

Proposition 4.1

(Existence with
L^{2}
velocity)
.

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfy (
4.1
), and let
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
be the unique solution to (
1.2
) associated with the lifted initial data
(\rho_{0}^{n},u_{0})
.
Then, up to a subsequence,
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
converges to a pair
(\rho,u)
which is a Leray-Hopf solution to (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
.

Moreover, the following convergences hold in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr)
:

(4.6)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u,\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}u,\qquad\dot{u}_{n}\to\dot{u},\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}.

Proof.

For the reader’s convenience we recall that, thanks to the uniform estimates (
4.5
), the following bounds hold uniformly in
n
and
t>0
:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+t\|\nabla u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+t^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}(t),\nabla^{2}u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+t^{3}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C,\\
&\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+s\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+s^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\Big)\,ds\leq C.\end{aligned}

Fix
T>0
and
0<\delta<T
.

Step 1.
Additional estimates for
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
.

Choose
R>0
sufficiently large. We claim that, for every
w
and almost every
t\in(0,T)

(4.8)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where the implicit constant may depend on
T
,
R
,
D
, and
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, but is independent of
n
.
We postpone the proof of (
4.8
) to the final step.

Choosing
w=u_{n}(t)
in (
4.8
) and multiplying by
\sqrt{t}
, we obtain

(4.9)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\,\|u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\sqrt{t}\,\|\nabla u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\sqrt{t}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Combining (
4.9
) with (
4.7
), we deduce, uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.10)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\,u_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((0,T);H^{1}(B_{R})\bigr),\qquad u_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((\delta,T);H^{2}(B_{R})\bigr).

Arguing in the same way with
w=\dot{u}_{n}(t)
, we infer

(4.11)

\displaystyle t\,\dot{u}_{n}\in L^{2}\bigl((0,T);H^{1}(B_{R})\bigr),\qquad\dot{u}_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((\delta,T);H^{1}(B_{R})\bigr).

We now estimate the time derivative
\partial_{t}u_{n}
, uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since

(4.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{n}=\dot{u}_{n}-(u_{n}\cdot\nabla)u_{n},

it remains to control the nonlinear term.

First, by Hölder’s inequality,

(4.13)

\displaystyle s\,\|(u_{n}(s)\cdot\nabla u_{n}(s))\|_{L^{1}(B_{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim s\,\|u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\bigl(\sqrt{s}\,\|u_{n}(s)\|_{H^{1}(B_{R})}\bigr)^{2},

and the right-hand side belongs to
L^{\infty}(0,T)
thanks to (
4.10
).

Moreover, for every
s\in(\delta,T)
,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\|\nabla[(u_{n}(s)\cdot\nabla)u_{n}(s)]\|_{L^{1}(B_{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}^{2}+\|u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\|\nabla^{2}u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})},

which is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\delta,T)
thanks to (
4.10
).

Combining these bounds with (
4.11
), we conclude that

(4.15)

\displaystyle t\,\partial_{t}u_{n}\in L^{2}\bigl((0,T);L^{1}(B_{R})\bigr),\qquad\partial_{t}u_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((\delta,T);W^{1,1}(B_{R})\bigr).

Step 2.
Local strong convergence of
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Let
R>0
and choose
\varepsilon\in\bigl(0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr)
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

(4.16)

\displaystyle v_{n}(t)\coloneqq t^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}u_{n}(t).

By (
4.10
) and (
4.15
),

(4.17)

\displaystyle v_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((0,T);H^{1}(B_{R})\bigr),\qquad\partial_{t}v_{n}\in L^{q}\bigl((0,T);L^{1}(B_{R})\bigr)

for some
q>1
.
Hence, by the Aubin–Lions lemma, up to a subsequence,

(4.18)

\displaystyle v_{n}\to v\quad\text{in }C\bigl([0,T];L^{2}(B_{R})\bigr).

Setting
u(t)\coloneqq t^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)}v(t)
, we infer that

(4.19)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u\quad\text{in }L^{1}\bigl((0,T);L^{2}(B_{R})\bigr).

By a diagonal argument, we conclude that

(4.20)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u\quad\text{in }L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Next, by
Lemma

3.7
(in particular (
3.37
)) with
v=u_{n}
, together with the uniform estimates in (
4.5
), there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, depending only on
T
,
D
, and
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
(uniformly in
n
), such that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(4.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}t^{3/4}\frac{|u_{n}(t,x)|}{1+|x|}\leq C^{\prime}.

Passing to the limit and using (
4.20
), we obtain

(4.22)

\displaystyle\frac{|u(t,x)|}{1+|x|}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{|u_{n}(t,x)|}{1+|x|}\leq C^{\prime}t^{-3/4}.

Since
t^{-3/4}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}[0,\infty)
, it follows that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\frac{|u(t,x)|}{1+|x|}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Then, by the DiPerna–Lions theory
[
16
, Theorem II.4.1]
, there exists
\rho\in C_{w^{*}}\bigl([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)

such that

(4.24)

\displaystyle\rho_{n}\to\rho\qquad\text{in }C\bigl([0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),\qquad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

By combining (
4.20
) and (
4.24
), and using that

\rho_{n}
is uniformly bounded, we obtain
2
2

2

The same argument also yields (
4.25
) with
\rho_{n}
in place of
\sqrt{\rho_{n}}
.

(4.25)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}\,u\qquad\text{in }L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Indeed, writing

(4.26)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}-\sqrt{\rho}\,u=\sqrt{\rho_{n}}(u_{n}-u)+(\sqrt{\rho_{n}}-\sqrt{\rho})u,

the first term converges by (
4.20
), while the second one is handled by truncating
u

and using the strong convergence of
\rho_{n}
in
C([0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}})
.
Thanks to (
4.20
) and (
4.25
) we obtain the first two convergences in (
4.6
).

Step 3.

(\rho,u)
is a Leray–Hopf weak solution.

Thanks to the uniform estimates (
4.7
) and the identification of the limit (
4.25
), we obtain

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \nabla u_{n}\rightharpoonup\nabla u&\qquad\text{in }L^{2}\bigl((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),\\
\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}\rightharpoonup^{*}\sqrt{\rho}\,u&\qquad\text{in }L^{\infty}\bigl((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).\end{aligned}

Together with (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), these convergences allow us to pass to the limit in the weak formulation of
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
.
Hence
(\rho,u)
is a weak solution.

To conclude that
(\rho,u)
is a Leray–Hopf solution, it remains to prove the energy inequality. The additional regularity given by (
4.10
) and (
4.15
) yields

(4.28)

\displaystyle u_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((\delta,T);H^{2}(B_{R})\bigr),\qquad\partial_{t}u_{n}\in L^{\infty}\bigl((\delta,T);W^{1,1}(B_{R})\bigr),

for every
R>0
.
By the Aubin–Lions lemma, up to extraction and a diagonal argument, we obtain

(4.29)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u\quad\text{in }C\bigl((0,\infty);H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),

which in particular implies

(4.30)

\displaystyle u_{n}\to u\quad\text{in }C\bigl((0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),\qquad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

Combining (
4.30
) with (
4.24
), we deduce

(4.31)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}\,u\qquad\text{in }C\bigl((0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)\qquad\forall p\in[1,\infty),

and thanks to (
4.27
) we also get

(4.32)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}(t)\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\rho}\,u(t)\qquad\text{weakly in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\forall t>0.

Using (
4.32
), (
4.27
), and the energy inequality satisfied by
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
, we infer that

(4.33)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho(t)}\,u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s

(4.34)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigg)

(4.35)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Step 4.
Weak convergences.

By (
4.29
), we have

(4.36)

\displaystyle(u_{n}\cdot\nabla)u_{n}\to(u\cdot\nabla)u\qquad\text{in }C\bigl((0,\infty);L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Consequently, we obtain the following identification:

(4.37)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{n}\to\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}).

This yields the third convergence in (
4.6
).
Moreover, by Banach–Alaoglu and the second estimate in (
4.11
), up to extraction of a subsequence and by a diagonal argument,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{n}\rightharpoonup^{*}f\qquad\text{in }L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl((0,\infty);H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

By uniqueness of the distributional limit, we conclude that
f=\dot{u}
.
In particular,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{n}\rightharpoonup^{*}\dot{u}\qquad\text{in }L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl((0,\infty);L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),\qquad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

Combining (
4.39
) with the strong convergence of the density (
4.24
), we conclude

(4.40)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}\to\sqrt{\rho}\,\dot{u}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}).

This concludes the convergences in (
4.6
).

Step 5.
Proof of (
4.8
).

Set

(4.41)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{n}\coloneqq\rho_{n}-\frac{1}{n}.

For every function
w
,

(4.42)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\widetilde{\rho}_{n}}\,w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Taking
w=u_{n}(t)
and using the energy inequality for
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
, we get

(4.43)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\widetilde{\rho}_{n}}\,u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq 2\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

The estimate (
4.43
) allows us to apply
Proposition

3.5
.
Since
\widetilde{\rho}_{n}
solves the transport equation with velocity field
u_{n}

and initial datum
\rho_{0}
,
Proposition

3.5
with
R=0
yields, for every
w

and every
x\in D
,

(4.44)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{L\sqrt{t}}(x))}\lesssim\sqrt{t}\,\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(B_{L\sqrt{t}}(x))}+\|\widetilde{\rho}_{n}(t)w\|_{L^{2}(B_{L\sqrt{t}}(x))},

where
L>0
depends only on
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.
By a covering argument and (
4.42
), we infer

(4.45)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(D)}\lesssim\sqrt{t}\,\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho_{n}(t)w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Fix
R_{0}>0
such that
D\subset B_{R_{0}}(0)
and let
R>R_{0}
.
Denote

(4.46)

\displaystyle w^{B_{R}}\coloneqq\fint_{B_{R}}w(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x,\qquad w^{D}\coloneqq\fint_{D}w(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

By Poincaré’s inequality,

(4.47)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\|w-w^{B_{R}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}+|B_{R}|^{1/2}|w^{B_{R}}|\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}+|w^{B_{R}}|.

Moreover,

(4.48)

\displaystyle|w^{B_{R}}|\leq|w^{B_{R}}-w^{D}|+|w^{D}|\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}+\|w\|_{L^{2}(D)}.

Combining the above estimates with (
4.45
), and using
t\in(0,T)
,
we conclude that

(4.49)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho_{n}(t)w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

∎

Remark 4.2
.

We recall that by (
4.31
) and (
4.27
), we obtain that for every
t>0
,

(4.50)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}(t)&\to\sqrt{\rho}\,u(t)&&\text{in }L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
\nabla u_{n}&\rightharpoonup\nabla u&&\text{in }L^{2}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr).\end{aligned}

However, the combination of these two convergences is not sufficient to pass to the limit in the energy equality satisfied by
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
. Indeed, in order to justify such a passage to the limit, one would need either a global version of the first convergence or a strong version of the second one, namely

(4.51)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,u_{n}(t)&\to\sqrt{\rho}\,u(t)&&\text{in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
\nabla u_{n}&\to\nabla u&&\text{in }L^{2}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr).\end{aligned}

Remark 4.3
.

The solution constructed in
Proposition

4.1
is indeed immediately strong.
To see this, recall the following general fact. Let
\alpha\geq 0
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, and

(4.52)

\displaystyle X=L^{p}\big((0,\infty),t^{\alpha}dt;L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big).

Assume that for some
m\in\mathbb{N}

(4.53)

\displaystyle f_{n}\to f\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\sup_{n}\|\nabla^{m}f_{n}\|_{X}<\infty.

Then
\nabla^{m}f\in X
and, up to a subsequence,

(4.54)

\displaystyle\nabla^{m}f_{n}\rightharpoonup\nabla^{m}f\quad\text{weakly (or weakly-$*$) in }X,

and

(4.55)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{m}f\|_{X}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\nabla^{m}f_{n}\|_{X}.

Applying this with
f_{n}=\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n}
,
u_{n}
,
\dot{u}_{n}
, and
\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n}
,
and using the distributional convergences in (
4.6
),
we obtain

(4.56)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u)\leq\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{n})\leq C,

where
C
is independent of
n
by (
4.5
).

Remark 4.4
.

In Section 2 we were able to show that smooth solutions also satisfy the following estimates uniformly in
t>0
:

(4.57)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{3}\|\nabla\dot{P},\sqrt{\rho}\,\ddot{u}\|_{2}\,ds\leq C.

Due to the lack of even higher-order estimates (such as
A_{4}
), it is unclear how to obtain sufficient compactness to show that non-smooth solutions also satisfy (
4.57
).

4.2.
Refined existence via atomic decomposition

We now aim to prove that the solution
(\rho,u)
constructed in
Proposition

4.1

satisfies the energy equality and that

(4.58)

\displaystyle\rho u\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

The argument is based on an atomic decomposition of the initial velocity.

Recall that if
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then for every
\eta\in(0,1)
there exists a sequence
(u_{0,j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in

\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

(4.59)

\displaystyle u_{0}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{0,j},\qquad\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2^{-j}\left\lVert u_{0,j}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+2^{j}\left\lVert u_{0,j}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\right)\leq C\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

For each atom
u_{0,j}
, we then consider the linearized system transported by
(\rho,u)
with initial datum
u_{0,j}
:

(4.60)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\rho u_{j})+\dive(\rho u\otimes u_{j})+\nabla P_{j}=\Delta u_{j},\\
\dive u_{j}=0,\\
u_{j}(0)=u_{0,j}.\end{cases}

In
Lemma

4.5
we prove that, for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a solution
u_{j}
to (
4.60
) such that

(4.61)

\displaystyle\rho u_{j}\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

and the corresponding energy equality holds. This is a consequence of the additional regularity of the initial data, namely

u_{0,j}\in\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Finally, in
Proposition

4.6
we show that the partial sums

(4.62)

\displaystyle\sum_{|j|\leq J}u_{j}

converge to
u
in a stronger sense with respect to the convergence of
u_{n}
to
u
. As a consequence,
u
inherits the same continuity and energy properties, which improves the conclusion of
Proposition

4.1
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
be as in (
4.1
), and let
(\rho,u)
be the solution to (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
constructed in
Proposition

4.1
.
For every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
u_{0,j}
be as in (
4.59
).

Then, for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists an immediately strong solution
u_{j}
to (
4.60
). Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
j
, such that

(4.63)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{j},u)

\displaystyle\leq C\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(4.64)

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{0,1,2,3\}}\sup_{t>0}A_{i}^{\eta}(t,u_{j},u)

\displaystyle\leq C\,\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

In addition,
\sqrt{\rho}\,u_{j}\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
u_{j}
satisfies the energy equality.

Finally, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
j
, such that for every
0<s<T<\infty
,

(4.65)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&\leq C\int_{s}^{T}\|\nabla u_{j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau,\\
\sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,\dot{u}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&\leq C\int_{s}^{T}\|\nabla u_{j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau,\\
\sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)^{3}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&\leq C\int_{s}^{T}\|\nabla u_{j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau.\end{aligned}

Proof.

We lift the initial density as in (
4.2
), obtaining a sequence of initial data
(\rho_{0}^{n},u_{0})
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the corresponding solution
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
arising from
(\rho_{0}^{n},u_{0})
.

Step 1.
Decay estimates.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we consider (
4.60
) with
\rho
and
u
replaced by
\rho_{n}
and
u_{n}
, and with initial datum
u_{0,j}
.
This yields a unique solution
u_{n,j}
.

By
Theorem

2.2
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, every
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, and every
t\in[0,\infty)
we have

(4.66)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{n,j},u_{n})&\leq C_{u_{n}}\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},\\
A_{i}^{1}(t,u_{n,j},u_{n})&\leq C_{u_{n}}\,\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\,\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Here we used that
C_{u_{n}}
depends in a nondecreasing way on
\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
which can be controlled by
\|u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.
Therefore
C_{u_{n}}
can be bounded by a constant
C
independent of
n
.

Since the a priori bounds for
u_{n,j}
are uniform in
n
and coincide with those available for
u_{n}
in (
4.5
), we can reproduce the proof of
Proposition

4.1
.
Hence the sequence
(\rho_{n},u_{n,j})
enjoys the same compactness properties as
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
in
Proposition

4.1
.
In particular, up to a subsequence, it converges to a pair
(\rho,u_{j})
which is an immediately strong solution to (
1.2
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0,j})
.

Moreover, the following convergences hold in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr)
:

(4.67)

\displaystyle u_{n,j}\to u_{j},\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}\to\sqrt{\rho}u_{j},\qquad\dot{u}_{n,j}\to\dot{u}_{j},\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{u}_{n,j}\to\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}_{j}.

Arguing as in
Remark

4.3
, we obtain

(4.68)

\displaystyle A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{j},u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}A_{i}^{0}(t,u_{n,j},u_{n})\leq C\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}=C\,\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},

where we used (
4.66
) and the convergence

(4.69)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{n}}\,u_{0,j}-\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=0.

This yields (
4.63
).

Similarly, passing to the limit in the estimate involving
\|u_{0,j}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, we obtain the corresponding
A_{i}^{1}
bound.
Since the constant
C
does not depend on
j
, interpolating between the
A_{i}^{0}
and
A_{i}^{1}
bounds yields (
4.64
).

Step 2.
Additional regularity.

Since
u_{0,j}\in\dot{H}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
we have the additional regularity of
A_{1}^{\eta}(t,u_{j},u)
. For instance we have

(4.70)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\eta-1}\|\nabla P_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,dt<\infty,

which implies

(4.71)

\displaystyle\nabla P_{j}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

Similarly, by the bounds on
A_{1}^{\eta}(\rho,u,u_{j})
and
A_{2}^{\eta}(\rho,u,u_{j})
we have

(4.72)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\to 0,\qquad\rho\dot{u}_{j},\;\sqrt{t}\,\nabla\dot{u}_{j}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

Moreover, by (
2.50
) and the bound on
A_{3}^{\eta}(\rho,u,u_{j})
we obtain

(4.73)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla^{2}u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{2-\eta}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1-\eta}{4}}t^{-\frac{3-\eta}{4}}.

Since
\eta>0
, the right-hand side belongs to
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}[0,\infty)
, and therefore

(4.74)

\displaystyle\nabla u_{j}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

Step 3.
Continuity in time and energy equality.

The regularity (
4.71
) allows us to test the momentum equation (
4.60
) against a non-divergence-free test function

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and obtain

(4.75)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)u_{j}(t)\cdot\varphi\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}u_{0,j}\cdot\varphi\,dx=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl[\rho u\otimes u_{j}+\nabla u_{j}\bigr]:\nabla\varphi+\nabla P_{j}\cdot\varphi\,dx\,d\tau.

Since

(4.76)

\displaystyle\rho u\otimes u_{j}+\nabla u_{j},\ \nabla P_{j}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

the right-hand side converges to
0
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Hence

(4.77)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}u_{j}(t)\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0,j}\qquad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

By the energy inequality for
u_{j}
the continuity is indeed strong

(4.78)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}u_{j}(t)\to\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0,j}\qquad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\quad\text{as }t\to 0^{+},

which proves the continuity at the initial time.

Collecting (
4.78
), (
4.72
), and (
4.74
), we see that all the assumptions of Scenario 2 in
Section

A.2
are satisfied with

(4.79)

\displaystyle(\rho_{1},u_{1})=(\rho_{2},u_{2})=(\rho,u),\qquad v_{1}=v_{2}=u_{j}

Hence, by
Proposition

A.7
, we obtain

(4.80)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)\,|u_{j}(t)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}\,|u_{0,j}|^{2}\,dx-2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}\,dx\,ds,

which yields the energy equality. This extends the continuity (
4.78
) to every time
t\geq 0
.

Step 4.
Strong convergence of the approximating sequence.

As shown in Step 3 of
Proposition

4.1
, see (
4.27
) and (
4.32
), for every
t>0
one has

(4.81)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\sqrt{\rho(t)}\,u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},\\
\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s&\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

On the other hand, by the energy equalities for both
(\rho_{n},u_{n,j})
and
(\rho,u_{j})
, together with (
4.69
), we obtain for every
t>0

(4.82)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lim_{n\to\infty}&\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigg)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho(t)}\,u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

Combining (
4.81
) and (
4.82
), we infer that for every
t>0

(4.83)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lim_{n\to\infty}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&=\|\sqrt{\rho(t)}\,u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},\\
\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s&=\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

Let

(4.84)

\displaystyle F_{n}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad F(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s.

Since
F_{n}
and
F
are continuous and nondecreasing, the pointwise convergence in (
4.83
) implies
F_{n}\to F
in
C([0,\infty))
. Using again the energy equalities, we then deduce that

(4.85)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(\cdot)}\,u_{n,j}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\to\|\sqrt{\rho(\cdot)}\,u_{j}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\qquad\text{in }C([0,\infty)).

Step 5.
Estimates (
4.65
).

To prove the first estimate, we recall that by (
2.16
) we have

(4.86)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\|\nabla u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Hence, for every
t>s>0
,

(4.87)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigl((t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\Bigr)\lesssim\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+(t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\|\nabla u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Applying Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain

(4.88)

\displaystyle(t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\lesssim\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau\exp\!\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau\right).

Since the exponential factor is uniformly bounded with respect to
n
and
j
, we deduce that for every
t>s\geq 0
,

(4.89)

\displaystyle(t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C_{u}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Then, similarly to
Remark

4.3
, we get for every
0<s<T<\infty

(4.90)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

(4.91)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in(s,T)}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau

(4.92)

\displaystyle=C_{u}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{s}^{T}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau

and by (
4.83
) we conclude

(4.93)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(s,T)}(t-s)\|\nabla u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C_{u}\int_{s}^{T}\|\nabla u_{j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau

which gives the first estimate in (
4.65
).

The other two estimates are obtained in the same way. For instance, it is enough to observe that

(4.94)

\displaystyle(t-s)^{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,\dot{u}_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}(t-s)^{2}\bigl(t-\tfrac{t+s}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\|\nabla u_{n,j}\bigl(\tfrac{t+s}{2}\bigr)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

(4.95)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}(t-s)\|\nabla u_{n,j}\bigl(\tfrac{t+s}{2}\bigr)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

(4.96)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}(t-s)\bigl(\tfrac{t+s}{2}-s\bigr)^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau

(4.97)

\displaystyle\leq C_{u}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla u_{n,j}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

One can then argue exactly as above.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
be as in (
4.1
), and let
(\rho,u)
be the solution to (
1.2
) arising from
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
constructed in
Proposition

4.1
.

For every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
u_{0,j}
be as in (
4.59
), and denote by
u_{j}
the solution to (
4.60
) arising from
u_{0,j}
constructed in
Lemma

4.5
. Then the partial sum
u_{J}
satisfies the following convergences:

(4.98)

\displaystyle\rho u_{J}\to\rho u\quad\text{in }C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\qquad\nabla u_{J}\to\nabla u\quad\text{in }L^{2}((0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

In particular,
u
satisfies the energy equality. Moreover, the following convergences hold in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr)
:

(4.99)

\displaystyle u_{J}\to u,\qquad\rho u_{J}\to\rho u,\qquad\dot{u}_{J}\to\dot{u},\qquad\rho\dot{u}_{J}\to\rho\dot{u}.

Remark 4.7
.

Proposition

4.6
shows that the approximating sequence
(u_{J})_{J}
enjoys better properties than
(u_{n})_{n}
. Indeed, the strong convergences in (
4.98
) are a novelty with respect to the approximation by lifting. According to
Remark

4.2
, this additional compactness allows us to pass to the limit in the energy equality for
(\rho,u_{J})
.

Proof.

First observe that, by (
4.59
), the partial sums

(4.100)

\displaystyle u_{0,J}:=\sum_{|j|\leq J}u_{0,j}

converge to
u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Since
u_{J}
is a finite sum of functions of the form
u_{j}
, it inherits all the properties established in
Lemma

4.5
. In particular,

(4.101)

\displaystyle\rho u_{J}\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

Hence, by
Theorem

A.1
, for almost every
t>0
,

(4.102)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)(u_{J}(t)-u(t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla(u_{J}(s)-u(s))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\|u_{0,J}-u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Since
u_{0,J}\to u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, from (
4.102
) we deduce (
4.98
) and the energy equality for
(\rho,u)
itself. We are left to show (
4.99
). We fix
T>0
and
R>0
.

By repeating the proof of (
4.8
)
3
3

3

It suffices to take
\rho_{n}=\tilde{\rho}_{n}=\rho
.
, we obtain that for every function
w
,

(4.103)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where the implicit constant depends only on
T
,
D
,
R
and
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.

Applying this estimate with
w=u_{J}(t)-u(t)
, we obtain

(4.104)

\displaystyle\|u_{J}(t)-u(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\|\nabla(u_{J}(t)-u(t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\rho(t)(u_{J}(t)-u(t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Using (
4.102
), the first term on the right-hand side tends to zero in
L^{2}(0,T)
, while the second one tends to zero in
L^{\infty}(0,T)
. Integrating in time over
(0,T)
, we conclude that

(4.105)

\displaystyle u_{J}\to u\qquad\text{in }L^{2}\bigl((0,T);L^{2}(B_{R})\bigr).

Since
T>0
and
R>0
are arbitrary, we obtain

(4.106)

\displaystyle u_{J}\to u\qquad\text{in }L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl([0,\infty);L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Together with the strong convergence of the gradient, this yields

(4.107)

\displaystyle(u_{J}\cdot\nabla)u_{J}\to(u\cdot\nabla)u\qquad\text{in }L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr),

and therefore

(4.108)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{J}\to\dot{u}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr).

To prove the convergence
\rho\dot{u}_{J}\to\rho\dot{u}
, we use (
4.103
) with

w=\dot{u}_{J}
. Multiplying by
t
, squaring and integrating, we obtain

(4.109)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\dot{u}_{J}(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{J}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\rho\dot{u}_{J}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
u_{J}=\sum_{|j|\leq J}u_{j}
, we estimate

(4.110)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\dot{u}_{J}(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sum_{|j|\leq J}\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\nabla\dot{u}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{|j|\leq J}\int_{0}^{T}t\|\rho\dot{u}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t.

By
Lemma

4.5
(see (
4.63
)), there exists a constant

C>0
, independent of
j
, such that

(4.111)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}t^{2}\|\dot{u}_{J}(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\sum_{|j|\leq J}\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\,u_{0,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Since the right-hand side is uniformly bounded in
J
, and by the
arbitrariness of
T
and
R
, we deduce that

(4.112)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{J}\to\dot{u}\qquad\text{in }L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr).

Consequently,

(4.113)

\displaystyle\rho\dot{u}_{J}\to\rho\dot{u}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr).

∎

4.3.
Backward parabolic system

We conclude this section with an existence result for a backward parabolic system.
Let
(\rho,u)
be the solution given by
Proposition

4.1
, and for every

j\in\mathbb{Z}
let
u_{j}
be the solution of (
4.60
) arising from the initial
data
u_{0,j}
, constructed in
Lemma

4.5
.

Given
T>0
, we consider the dual system of (
4.60
) on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(4.114)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\rho w_{j}^{T})+\dive(\rho u\otimes w_{j}^{T})+\nabla Q_{j}^{T}=-\Delta w_{j}^{T}+\rho u_{j},\\
\dive w_{j}^{T}=0,\\
w_{j}^{T}(T)=0.\end{cases}

Lemma 4.8
.

In the above setting, there exists a distributional solution
w_{j}^{T}
to
(
4.114
) and a constant
C>0
, depending only on

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, such that

(4.115)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}(T-t)^{-1}\|\sqrt{\rho}\,w_{j}^{T}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\nabla w_{j}^{T}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Moreover,

(4.116)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho u_{j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}(x)\,w_{j}^{T}(0,x)\cdot u_{0,j}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x,

and for every
t\in[0,T]
,

(4.117)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(t)\,w_{j}^{T}(t)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=-(T-t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}u_{0,j}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
w
in place of
w_{j}^{T}
.
As discussed in
Proposition

4.1
, there exists a lifting approximation
scheme
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
converging to
(\rho,u)
.
Moreover, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a unique solution

u_{n,j}
of (
4.60
), advected by
(\rho_{n},u_{n})
, with initial data

u_{0,j}
.
By
Lemma

4.5
,
u_{n,j}
converges to
u_{j}
, which solves (
4.60
)
advected by
(\rho,u)
with initial data
u_{0,j}
.

We denote by
w_{n}
the solution of

(4.118)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}(\rho_{n}w_{n})+\dive(\rho_{n}u_{n}\otimes w_{n})+\nabla Q^{T}_{n,j}=-\Delta w_{n}+\rho_{n}u_{n,j},\\
\dive w_{n}=0,\\
w_{n}(T)=0.\end{cases}

Step 1.
A priori estimates. Testing the momentum equation in (
4.118
) with
w_{n}
yields

(4.119)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{n}u_{n,j}(t)\cdot w_{n}(t)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Integrating in time over
(t,T)
and using Cauchy–Schwarz, we obtain

(4.120)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}(t)\,w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}&\leq\int_{t}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq(T-t)^{1/2}\left(\int_{t}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}.\end{aligned}

We next test the momentum equation in (
4.118
) with
\dot{w}_{n}
and obtain

(4.121)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{w}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}-\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla w_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}&=\langle\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j},\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{w}_{n}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\nabla w_{n},\nabla\big((u_{n}\cdot\nabla)w_{n}\big)\rangle+\langle Q^{T}_{n,j},\dive\dot{w}_{n}\rangle\\
&\eqqcolon a^{T}_{n,j}(t).\end{aligned}

As in
Section

2
(see (
2.16
)), the right-hand side can be estimated as

(4.122)

\displaystyle|a^{T}_{n,j}(t)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{w}_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+C\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+C\|\nabla u_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},

with a constant
C
independent of
T
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Inserting (
4.122
) into (
4.121
), absorbing the term

\frac{1}{2}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{w}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}
on the left-hand side, and integrating from
t
to
T
, we obtain, for every
t\in(0,T]
,

(4.123)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\dot{w}_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}

(4.124)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{t}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{t}^{T}\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\|\nabla w_{n}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By Grönwall’s inequality, we conclude that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

(4.125)

\displaystyle\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\int_{t}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}u_{n,j}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Step 2.
Compactness and convergence.

Combining (
4.120
) and (
4.125
), we obtain

(4.126)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\Big(\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}}\,w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\Big)\leq C.

Applying (
4.8
) with
w=w_{n}(t)
, we infer that for every sufficiently large
R>0
,

(4.127)

\displaystyle\|w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(B_{R})}\lesssim\|\nabla w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\sqrt{\rho_{n}(t)}\,w_{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where the implicit constant may depend on
T
,
R
,
D
, and
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.
Combining (
4.127
) with (
4.126
), we obtain

(4.128)

\displaystyle\|w_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T);H^{1}(B_{R}))}\leq C.

Therefore, up to a subsequence and by a diagonal argument,

(4.129)

\displaystyle w_{n}\rightharpoonup^{*}w\qquad\text{in }L^{\infty}\bigl((0,T);H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr).

Moreover, by (
4.24
) and (
4.25
) (applied to both
u_{n}
and
u_{n,j}
) we have

(4.130)
