\left\lvert v_{i}(t,x)\right\rvert=O(1)
as
\left\lvert x\right\rvert\to\infty
. We may therefore apply
Corollary

3.14
to
(\rho_{1},u_{1})
with
v=v_{1}(t)
and
h(t)=v_{2}(t)
. After integrating over
({\varepsilon},t)
, we obtain

(A.64)

\displaystyle 0=-\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,\dot{v}_{1}\cdot v_{2}-\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v_{1}:\nabla v_{2},

and, in the same way,

(A.65)

\displaystyle 0=-\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{2}\,\dot{v}_{2}\cdot v_{1}-\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v_{1}:\nabla v_{2}.

A crucial step is to test the weak formulation of the transport equation associated with
(\rho_{1},u_{1})
against the function
v_{1}\cdot v_{2}
and to obtain

(A.66)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}(t)\,v_{1}(t)\cdot v_{2}(t)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}(\varepsilon)\,v_{1}(\varepsilon)\cdot v_{2}(\varepsilon)=\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,v_{2}\cdot\dot{v}_{1}+\rho_{1}\,v_{1}\cdot D_{u_{1}}v_{2}.

This identity is precisely (
A.11
) with the corresponding replacements.

As in the proof of
Corollary

A.3
, the main difficulty in establishing (
A.66
) is to verify that
v_{1}\cdot v_{2}
is an admissible test function. The argument follows the same lines as in
Corollary

A.3
; however, since
v_{2}
is not advected by
(\rho_{1},u_{1})
, quantities such as
\rho_{1}v_{2}
or
\rho_{1}D_{u_{1}}v_{2}
may a priori fail to have the required spatial integrability.

This issue can be handled exactly as in the proof of
Lemma

A.6
, by repeatedly exploiting localized
L^{p}
bounds for
v_{2}
and
D_{u_{1}}v_{2}
. As a representative example, for almost every
t>0
one has

(A.67)

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}(t)\,v_{2}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\|v_{2}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{N,\sqrt{t}})}\lesssim t^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla v_{2}(t)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho_{2}(t)\,v_{2}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Similar estimates apply to all the other terms arising in the weak formulation, and ensure that
v_{1}\cdot v_{2}
is an admissible test function.

Once (
A.66
) is established, we add (
A.64
), (
A.65
), and (
A.66
) to obtain

(A.68)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}(t)\,v_{1}(t)\cdot v_{2}(t)-&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}({\varepsilon})\,v_{1}({\varepsilon})\cdot v_{2}({\varepsilon})\\
=&\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}v_{1}\cdot D_{u_{1}}v_{2}-\rho_{2}v_{1}\cdot D_{u_{2}}v_{2}-2\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v_{1}:\nabla v_{2}.\end{aligned}

Step 2.
We now turn to a different identity, following a similar strategy. The density difference
\delta\rho:=\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}
satisfies

(A.69)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta\rho+\dive(\delta\rho\,u_{2})=-\dive(\rho_{1}\,\delta u),

in the sense of distributions. Therefore, provided that
\lvert v_{2}\rvert^{2}/2
is an admissible test function on compact subsets of
(0,\infty)
, we obtain

(A.70)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho(t)\,\lvert v_{2}(t)\rvert^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho(\varepsilon)\,\lvert v_{2}(\varepsilon)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,v_{2}\cdot\partial_{t}v_{2}+\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,u_{2}\cdot\nabla\!\left(\frac{\lvert v_{2}\rvert^{2}}{2}\right)

(A.71)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,\delta u\cdot\nabla\!\left(\frac{\lvert v_{2}\rvert^{2}}{2}\right).

After straightforward manipulations, this identity yields

(A.72)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho(t)\,\lvert v_{2}(t)\rvert^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho({\varepsilon})\,\lvert v_{2}({\varepsilon})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,v_{2}\cdot D_{u_{2}}v_{2}+\rho_{1}\,\delta u\otimes v_{2}:\nabla v_{2}.

As before, the only nontrivial point is to verify that
\lvert v_{2}\rvert^{2}/2
is an admissible test function for (
A.69
). In this case, the required
L^{p}
bounds for the products
\delta\rho\,v_{2}
and
\delta\rho\,\dot{v}_{2}
are again obtained by exploiting the finite speed of propagation of the density, namely

(A.73)

\displaystyle\supp\delta\rho(t)\subset D+B_{N\sqrt{t}}(0),

and by repeating the localized estimates described above in (
A.67
).

Step 3.
Subtracting (
A.72
) from (
A.68
), we obtain

(A.74)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(t)\,v_{1}(t)\cdot v_{2}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho(t)\,|v_{2}(t)|^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}({\varepsilon})\,v_{1}({\varepsilon})\cdot v_{2}({\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho({\varepsilon})\,|v_{2}({\varepsilon})|^{2}

(A.75)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\underbrace{-\rho_{1}v_{1}\cdot D_{u_{1}}v_{2}+\rho_{2}v_{1}\cdot D_{u_{2}}v_{2}+\delta\rho\,v_{2}\cdot D_{u_{2}}v_{2}+\rho_{1}\,\delta u\otimes v_{2}:\nabla v_{2}}_{=:K}+2\int_{\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v_{1}:\nabla v_{2}.

By straightforward algebraic manipulations, we can rewrite

(A.76)

\displaystyle K=-\rho_{1}\,\delta u\otimes\delta v:\nabla v_{2}-\delta\rho\,\delta v\cdot D_{u_{2}}v_{2}.

We now study the limit as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. By (
A.41
), we have

(A.77)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}({\varepsilon})\,v_{1}({\varepsilon})\cdot v_{2}({\varepsilon})\,dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho({\varepsilon})\,|v_{2}({\varepsilon})|^{2}\,dx\longrightarrow-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}v_{1}(0)\cdot v_{2}(0)\,dx.

For the term
K
, we estimate

(A.78)

\displaystyle\|K\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\|\rho_{1}\,\delta u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\|\rho_{1}\,\delta v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\|\nabla v_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\delta\rho\,\delta v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\|\delta\rho\dot{v}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which belongs to
L^{1}(0,t)
by
Lemma

A.6
and the assumptions on
v_{2}
(the inclusion
\rho_{1}\,\delta u\in L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
can be proved by choosing
v=\delta u(t)
and
i=1
in (
A.44
)). Since
\nabla v_{1},\nabla v_{2}\in L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
, by dominated convergence we may pass to the limit as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, and we obtain

(A.79)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}(t)\,v_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\cdot v_{2}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho(t)\,|v_{2}(t)|^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{0}v_{1}(0)\cdot v_{2}(0)

(A.80)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{1}\,\delta u\otimes\delta v:\nabla v_{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta\rho\,\delta v\cdot\dot{v}_{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla v_{1}:\nabla v_{2}.

∎
